# BFS evidence map

This document is a claim ledger, not another chronological log.  It separates
what is true by definition or proof from what was merely observed in one model,
what has been falsified as a universal claim, and what remains unknown.

The scope column is essential.  A result on adjacent-transposition `S_8`, an RTX
3070 Laptop GPU, or a synthetic key distribution is not silently promoted to a
law about BFS.

Correction pass, 2026-08-31: affected rows are corrected in place without
renumbering IDs. The dated [audit](reviews/2026-08-31-bfs-research-audit.md)
preserves the old claims. In particular, the formerly rejected K1/K2 first-hit
claim is true under its exact-complete-ball premises. This ledger presents
corrected claims, not a uniformly independently verified corpus.

## Status vocabulary

- **Definition** — meaning adopted by this project.
- **Proved fact** — follows from stated mathematical assumptions.
- **Qualified fact** — proved under explicitly listed conditions and false or
  unproved when those conditions are removed.
- **Validated fact** — checked exhaustively or by a retained correctness oracle
  over the named finite scope.
- **Observation** — directly measured in a retained experiment.
- **Inference** — explanation supported by facts and assumptions but not itself
  exhaustively established.
- **Rejected universal** — a counterexample disproves an "always" claim.
- **Unknown** — current evidence is insufficient.

## Semantic core

| ID | Status | Claim | Assumptions and scope | Evidence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| SEM-01 | Definition | Exact ordinary BFS computes hop distance from a source set. | Unit-cost directed or undirected graph; exact adopted vertex identity. | Notes 01, 03, 05 |
| SEM-02 | Proved fact | `B_(d+1)=B_d union N(B_d)` and `F_d=B_d minus B_(d-1)` characterize the reachable metric balls and layers. | `N` is the complete outgoing-neighbor operator. | Metric-ball induction in note 03 |
| SEM-03 | Proved fact | A queue is a schedule, not the semantic result; a schedule is correct only if it preserves nondecreasing finalization or permits sound relaxation. | Depends on the finalization rule. | Note 03 and the stack counterexample |
| SEM-04 | Proved fact | A false-positive visited decision can destroy completeness. | The aliased state may be the only entrance to an unreached region. | Hash-collision counterexample, notes 03/04/06 |
| SEM-05 | Proved fact | Distances and complete frontier sets are independent of within-layer order, but parents and work can depend on it. | Complete exact level processing. | Note 04 |
| SEM-06 | Proved fact | Multi-source BFS computes distance to the source set; source ownership may be tie-dependent. | Every source initialized at depth zero. | Note 05 |
| SEM-07 | Proved fact | Backward bidirectional search on a directed graph must follow reversed edges. | The backward label means distance to the target. | Note 05; exhaustive local validation in REF-007 |
| SEM-08 | Qualified fact | Starting with disjoint exact discovered balls, the first intersection during expansion of one next layer is shortest if the opposite visited set remains that fixed exact ball; finishing the active layer is unnecessary for one distance/path. | Unit edges, correct reverse graph, exact checking. Arbitrary interleaving of partial layers on both sides does not inherit this guarantee. | Proof and clarified boundaries in note 08 |
| SEM-09 | Proved fact | Beam pruning removes ordinary BFS completeness and shortest-path guarantees. | A discarded frontier state can contain the only or shortest solution. | Note 05 counterexample |
| SEM-10 | Proved fact | 0-1 BFS is relaxation-based weighted shortest path, not ordinary irrevocable first discovery. | Edge costs restricted to zero and one. | Note 05; relation to Dial/Dijkstra |
| SEM-11 | Proved fact | For exact complete-level bidirectional BFS with minimum unexpanded depths `a,b` and best feasible path `mu`, `a+b>=mu` proves optimality. | Unit-cost graph; backward side traverses the reverse graph; intersections are not missed. | Note 08; implementation validated in REF-007 |
| SEM-12 | Proved fact | Distance-optimal stopping need not enumerate all shortest parents, crossing edges, or meeting vertices. | Output requires more than one shortest distance/path. | Note 08 |
| SEM-13 | Proved fact | On an effectively presented locally finite graph, BFS eventually finds every reachable finite-depth target. | Finite source set, terminating complete successor enumeration, exact decidable identity, fair schedule, no loss. | Finite-ball proof in note 09 |
| SEM-14 | Proved fact | Full BFS traversal terminates when the reachable component is finite; solution completeness alone does not imply termination on unreachable targets in an infinite component. | Exact graph search and terminating expansion. | Infinite-ray counterexample in note 09 |
| SEM-15 | Proved fact | Infinite branching can prevent a strict layer-synchronous BFS from completing a finite layer and reaching a deeper target. | Infinite successor enumeration at a shallower vertex. | Root-with-countably-many-children counterexample in note 09 |
| SEM-16 | Definition | `FOUND`, `EXHAUSTED`, `BOUNDED`, and `INCOMPLETE` are distinct run outcomes. | `EXHAUSTED` requires a full-component certificate; limits alone yield only bounded/incomplete results. | Note 09 |
| SEM-17 | Proved fact | Frontier width is the outer vertex boundary of the visited ball; generated occurrences, unique candidates, visited hits, and accepted next states are distinct counts linked by a conservation identity. | Complete exact level and adopted directed out-neighbor relation. | Note 10 and REF-003 |
| SEM-18 | Rejected universal | Vertex degree or branching factor alone predicts BFS frontier growth. | Relations, convergence, visited history, bottlenecks, and finite saturation can dominate. | Tree/lattice/clique counterexamples in note 10 |
| SEM-19 | Proved fact | Exact distances, one shortest-path tree, the shortest-path DAG, and explicit all-path enumeration are different output objects. | Unit-cost graph with a declared path identity for labels/parallel edges. | Note 11 |
| SEM-20 | Proved fact | Shortest-path counts satisfy a depth-ordered predecessor recurrence, while the number of paths can be exponential in the DAG size. | Exact predecessor edges and nonoverflowing declared arithmetic. | Layered counterexample and Brandes recurrence in note 11 |
| SEM-21 | Proved fact | A valid parent chain alone does not prove shortestness; complete edge distance inequalities provide the complementary bound. | Complete reachable graph result. | Note 11 and REF-001 negative fixture |
| SEM-22 | Qualified fact | Same-child candidate records are redundant for next-frontier membership but may be required as distinct shortest predecessors or labeled paths. | Depends on the requested output and path identity. | Note 11 |
| SEM-23 | Proved fact | Ordinary BFS first discovery is final because all unit-edge proposals arrive in nondecreasing hop layers; 0-1 weights invalidate that proof. | Exact nondecreasing ordinary BFS schedule versus weighted relaxation. | Three-edge counterexample and proof in note 12 |
| SEM-24 | Proved fact | 0-1 BFS is a two-adjacent-bucket relaxation algorithm; a discovered vertex may need a smaller label and reactivation. | Edge costs in `{0,1}` and correct deque/stale-entry policy. | Note 12; relation to Dial buckets |
| SEM-25 | Qualified fact | The shortest-predecessor structure is a DAG when every shortest predecessor edge strictly increases distance; zero-cost edges can create equal-distance cycles. | Strictly positive versus zero edge costs. | Note 12 |
| SEM-26 | Rejected universal | Queue/deque membership means a vertex distance is already final. | True under ordinary BFS first discovery, false for tentative 0-1/label-correcting entries. | Note 12 counterexample |
| SEM-27 | Proved fact | Joint multi-source BFS computes the pointwise minimum distance to the source set, not a separate distance vector for every source. | All distinct sources seeded at depth zero with unit real edges. | Note 13 |
| SEM-28 | Qualified fact | Scalar nearest-source distance is canonical, while a single nearest-source/Voronoi label requires arbitrary, canonical, or set-valued tie semantics. | Ties exist when several sources attain the same minimum. | Note 13 and graph Voronoi model |
| SEM-29 | Proved fact | A canonical equal-distance source-label improvement may need downstream propagation even when the vertex distance was already final. | Tie rule orders source labels and descendants inherit labels. | Note 13 |
| SEM-30 | Rejected universal | Multi-source semantic labels and physical rank/GPU ownership are the same partition. | One represents proximity; the other represents storage/authority/load distribution. | Note 13 |
| SEM-31 | Proved fact | Exact push and pull BFS compute the same next-frontier predicate when edge orientation, membership, universe enumeration, and level snapshot agree. | Unit edges and lossless complete-level execution. | Proof in note 14 |
| SEM-32 | Rejected universal | Invertible generators are sufficient to support conventional pull BFS. | Pull also needs an enumerable unvisited universe and exact frontier membership. | Note 14 and implicit-state counterexample |
| SEM-33 | Qualified fact | Pull may stop at the first frontier predecessor for distance/one arbitrary parent, but must inspect more for canonical or all-predecessor outputs. | Output contract determines required witnesses. | Note 14 |
| SEM-34 | Rejected universal | Pull is always cheaper because it avoids duplicate child claims. | It may scan many unvisited vertices and failed predecessor edges. | Path/dense/bottleneck counterexamples in note 14 |
| SEM-35 | Proved fact | Bulk sorting, partitioning, and delayed duplicate detection preserve BFS layers when they exactly realize `unique(expand(F_d)) \\ B_d` before expansion of the result. | Unit edges, exact state identity, complete level contribution. | Set derivation in note 15 |
| SEM-36 | Qualified fact | Physical visited may be updated with exact next-layer claims during a level without changing scalar BFS distances. | Claimed states are not expanded until the next level; richer outputs need extra handling. | Early-commit counterexample in note 15 |
| SEM-37 | Rejected universal | Local/batch deduplication replaces global visited and duplicate detection. | The same state may occur in another batch, partition, or producer. | Two-duplicate taxonomy in note 15 |
| SEM-38 | Rejected universal | A probabilistic visited filter with final false-positive rejection is exact BFS. | False positives can remove the only route to a reachable state. | Filter-role analysis in note 15 |
| SEM-39 | Qualified fact | External BFS over implicit/Cayley states does not require a dense rank. | It does require canonical serialization and exact collision handling; rank strongly affects physical cost. | Implicit/Cayley analysis in note 15 |
| SEM-40 | Proved fact | For right Cayley edges `g -> g*s`, arbitrary-source distance normalizes as `dist(x,y)=length_S(x^-1*y)`. | Same generator alphabet and exact group-element vertices. | Left-translation proof in note 16 |
| SEM-41 | Proved fact | A transitive right action with base stabilizer `H` has state vertices represented by right cosets `Hg`, forming `H\G`. | Right action convention; `gH` would be a left coset. | Coset derivation in note 16 |
| SEM-42 | Rejected universal | Every puzzle orbit graph normalizes from `start=x0*a` to the single Cayley target `a^-1*b`. | With nontrivial stabilizer, valid words form `a^-1*H*b`. | Schreier target derivation in note 16 |
| SEM-43 | Rejected universal | An algebraically invertible move automatically provides the opposite edge in the original forward graph. | Inverse closure is sufficient for forward symmetry; reverse search instead uses predecessors even if inverse labels are not forward-allowed. | Directed `Z` and move-set counterexamples in note 16; note 08 |
| SEM-44 | Qualified fact | Cayley parity descends to a Schreier state parity exactly when the stabilizer lies in the parity homomorphism kernel. | A parity homomorphism mapping all generators to one is defined. | Representative-independence proof in note 16 |
| SEM-45 | Proved fact | A graph homomorphism/quotient gives `dist_Q(pi(s),pi(t)) <= dist_G(s,t)` but does not alone guarantee path lifting. | Unit edge projection; quotient may collapse internal edges. | Projection proof and witness mismatch in note 17 |
| SEM-46 | Proved fact | An automorphism-orbit quotient computes distance from a concrete source to the nearest representative of a target orbit. | Automorphisms preserve direction/adjacency and quotient edges use orbit witnesses. | Two-sided lift/projection proof in note 17 |
| SEM-47 | Rejected universal | Symmetry quotienting preserves distance to a fixed target representative. | It preserves target-orbit distance unless stronger endpoint conditions hold. | Reflected path counterexample in note 17 |
| SEM-48 | Qualified fact | A covering projection uniquely lifts a quotient path after its concrete start is fixed. | Local bijection/covering condition. | Path-lifting theorem in note 17 |
| SEM-49 | Rejected universal | A parent chain over canonical states is automatically replay-valid in the original puzzle. | Symmetries may rename moves and consecutive quotient edges may use incompatible representatives. | Frame/lifting analysis in note 17 |
| SEM-50 | Proved fact | Irrevocable first discovery with an arbitrary asynchronous schedule can finalize a non-shortest label. | Unit directed graph; longer causal chain runs first. | Stack/asynchronous counterexample in notes 03/18 |
| SEM-51 | Proved fact | Fair asynchronous unit-edge relaxation converges to BFS distances at global quiescence. | Exact minimum, witness proposals, eventual propagation, no loss, finite/convergent scope. | Two-sided fixed-point proof in note 18 |
| SEM-52 | Rejected universal | Applying `atomicMin` to distance is sufficient without reactivating a vertex after improvement. | Better labels must propagate to descendants. | `x -> y` counterexample in note 18 |
| SEM-53 | Rejected universal | Simultaneously empty local queues prove distributed asynchronous termination. | Messages or producer work may still be in flight. | Quiescence analysis in note 18 |
| SEM-54 | Qualified fact | A stale relaxation proposal may be semantically safe because it still witnesses a real path. | It cannot overwrite a better label or suppress propagation of the winning decrease. | Stale-work analysis in note 18 |
| SEM-55 | Proved fact | Ordered FIFO BFS yields shortlex-least state words when complete parent prefixes and outgoing labels are processed in lexicographic order. | Unit labeled graph, total alphabet/tie order, exact first-occurrence dedup. | Layer induction in note 19 |

| SEM-63 | Rejected universal | A simple shortest path in the product graph projects to a simple path in the base graph. | The projection may revisit one base vertex under distinct memory states. | Product projection analysis in note 20 |
| SEM-64 | Qualified fact | Cayley-product BFS computes the shortest generator word representing the target and accepted by the constraint automaton. | Exact group/product identity and target acceptance contract. | Cayley product derivation in note 20 |

| SEM-74 | Qualified fact | A mixed-version edge sequence may be invalid for every snapshot yet valid as a temporal journey. | Edge timestamps are nondecreasing and temporal semantics allow the traversal. | Snapshot/temporal counterexample in note 22 |

| SEM-85 | Rejected universal | An exact local BFS lookup makes a surrounding beam-search pipeline exact BFS. | The table certifies only its covered subproblem/suffix; discarded global prefixes remain unexamined. | Note 24 and beam-search literature |

| SEM-87 | Proved fact | `B_d` in the cumulative BFS recurrence is exactly the distance-at-most-d ball, and its delta `F_d` is the exact distance-d shell. | Fixed directed unweighted graph and source set. | Induction in note 25 |
| SEM-88 | Proved fact | Expanding only the newest BFS delta is equivalent to re-expanding the accumulated ball and subtracting visited. | Relational `Post` distributes over unions; exact set difference. | Delta derivation in note 25 |
| SEM-89 | Proved fact | Finite-path reachability is the least fixed point of `T(X)=S union Post(X)`, including on infinite graphs. | Powerset lattice; relational image preserves arbitrary unions. | Least-fixed-point proof in note 25 |
| SEM-90 | Rejected universal | Monotonicity alone guarantees that countable iteration from bottom reaches the least fixed point and supports the BFS delta recurrence. | An explicit monotone operator adds `infinity` only at stage `omega+1`; it is not union-preserving. | Counterexample in note 25 |
| SEM-91 | Qualified fact | Local finiteness is unnecessary for the set-theoretic BFS distance invariant but sufficient, with finite sources, for finite frontiers at every finite depth. | Full traversal of an infinite reachable graph may still never terminate. | Finite/infinite distinction in note 25 |
| SEM-92 | Proved fact | After a complete synchronous layer, an empty next frontier proves successor closure and reachable-set termination. | All producers complete, exact visited/filtering, no loss or truncation. | Closure argument in note 25 |
| SEM-93 | Rejected universal | Fairness or local queue emptiness by itself proves distributed BFS termination. | Fairness supports progress; global termination also requires no local work and no messages in flight on a valid protocol cut. | Quiescence analysis in note 25 |
| SEM-94 | Proved fact | A physical superstep containing k exact ordered microlevels can preserve the original BFS distances. | Minimum original depths, complete logical sublevels, cross-owner consequences, and witnesses are retained. | Note 26 |
| SEM-95 | Proved fact | BFS on the at-most-k power graph reports `ceil(dist_G/k)`, not the original move distance. | Macro-edge exists for every directed walk of length 1 through k. | Two-sided distance proof in note 26 |
| SEM-96 | Rejected universal | Exact-k Boolean matrix powers represent all vertices reachable within k edges. | `A^k` represents walks of exactly k; a length-two chain with k=2 omits its depth-one vertex. | Counterexample in note 26 |
| SEM-97 | Rejected universal | Atomic first-wins visited remains distance-correct when several logical depths race inside one batch. | A depth-three arrival can suppress an existing depth-two path. | Mixed-depth counterexample in note 26 |

| SEM-99 | Rejected universal | Delaying all communication until the end of a local k-hop expansion preserves a distributed implicit BFS. | A path may change owner at an intermediate hop and require the remote owner to continue it. | Cross-owner argument in note 26 |
| SEM-100 | Proved fact | A macro-parent endpoint pair alone is insufficient to replay an original labeled path. | Needs stored intermediates/labels or an exact validated witness regeneration rule. | Note 26 |
| SEM-101 | Proved fact | If `2r<girth`, every vertex in a root ball of radius r has a unique shortest root path. | Simple undirected graph; sharp on even cycles. | Two-path cycle proof in note 27 |
| SEM-102 | Proved fact | If `2r+1<girth`, the subgraph induced by a root ball of radius r is a tree. | Simple undirected graph; sharp on odd cycles. | Extra-edge cycle proof in note 27 |
| SEM-103 | Rejected universal | BFS duplicates begin only when the graph girth is reached. | Immediate inverse backtracking occurs even in an infinite tree and is not a simple cycle. | Note 27 |
| SEM-104 | Proved fact | The odd/even Moore bounds are collision-free BFS tree counts around a vertex or an edge. | Finite simple undirected q-regular graph of stated girth. | Note 27 and cage literature |

| SEM-106 | Rejected universal | The shortest relator explicitly listed in one presentation equals the Cayley graph girth. | Relators may reduce/repeat, consequences may be shorter, and generator changes alter the graph. | Note 27 |
| SEM-107 | Proved fact | A closed Schreier-state word need only lie in a stabilizer, not equal the group identity. | Fixed action and left/right convention. | Note 27 |
| SEM-108 | Proved fact | A false-positive visited decision can delete the only path to a reachable target. | Hash-only collision between a dead-end state and the target's gateway. | Minimal graph witness in note 28 |
| SEM-109 | Qualified fact | A false-negative visited decision is extra work only when an exact later stage repairs identity before it affects distances, capacity, counts, or termination. | Output- and schedule-dependent. | Note 28 |
| SEM-110 | Proved fact | An injective semantic rank makes a dense bitmap an exact visited set. | Injectivity over every queried valid state and nontruncating arithmetic. | Note 28 |
| SEM-111 | Proved fact | An ordinary hash table can be exact despite collisions. | Full/injective keys, complete probing, exact equality, and explicit capacity behavior. | Note 28 |
| SEM-112 | Rejected universal | A wide or multiword fingerprint is exact state identity because collision probability is negligible. | A many-to-one map remains probabilistic without an injectivity proof. | Pigeonhole argument in note 28 |
| SEM-113 | Rejected universal | A minimal perfect hash built for a static set automatically recognizes every unseen BFS state exactly. | Nonmembers may map to occupied slots; domain membership or exact key checking is still needed. | Note 28 and FKS scope |
| SEM-114 | Rejected universal | A standalone Bloom filter is an exact BFS visited set. | False positives can prune genuinely new gateway states. | Note 28 and Bloom's allowable-error contract |
| SEM-115 | Proved fact | Hash collisions are harmless for owner routing if equal states route consistently and the owner performs exact equality. | Stable canonical key/epoch and authoritative collision resolution. | Note 28 |
| SEM-116 | Qualified fact | Full exact BFS over a finite explicit adjacency-list component uses `Theta(|R|+A_R)` work. | Constant-time visited/key operations and one inspection per stored outgoing adjacency entry. | Note 29 |
| SEM-117 | Proved fact | Full component certification has a worst-case `Omega(A_R)` adjacency-inspection requirement in the explicit-list access model. | An uninspected outgoing entry could hide another reachable vertex. | Adversary argument in note 29 |
| SEM-118 | Rejected universal | `O(V+E)` is a complete cost model for implicit/Cayley BFS. | Move generation, legality, wide-state identity, and candidate multiplicity are not represented by a prebuilt edge list. | Note 29 |
| SEM-119 | Rejected universal | `O(b^d)` equals graph-BFS work for branching factor b and target depth d. | It describes an unmerged path-tree bound; convergence, relations, bottlenecks, and saturation change graph work. | Note 29 |
| SEM-120 | Proved fact | Materializing all reached distances or one parent per reached state has an `Omega(|R|)` output-size lower bound. | Explicit per-state output requested. | Note 29 |
| SEM-121 | Qualified fact | Frontier queue space can be `O(max_d |F_d|)` while total exact BFS state remains `O(|R|)` because visited/output persist. | Constant-size records; candidate and scratch peaks accounted separately. | Note 29 |
| SEM-122 | Rejected universal | Graph500 TEPS is the number of adjacency inspections actually executed per second. | Its numerator is normalized traversed-component input edges; implementations may inspect different work. | Graph500 specification and note 29 |
| SEM-123 | Proved fact | For q total generators acting everywhere, a complete finite Cayley traversal attempts `q|R|` labeled transition occurrences. | Every reached state, including the last frontier, is expanded to prove exhaustion. | Note 29 |
| SEM-124 | Rejected universal | Persisting visited membership alone is sufficient for exact BFS recovery. | A crash after visited claim but before durable frontier enqueue creates an unexpanded orphan state. | Three-vertex witness in note 30 |
| SEM-125 | Proved fact | A clean completed-level checkpoint can resume exact BFS from its accumulated ball and unexpanded newest frontier. | No earlier-level work/messages in flight; graph and state semantics unchanged. | Note 30 |
| SEM-126 | Qualified fact | At-least-once candidate delivery can preserve reached-set BFS without physical exactly-once execution. | Exact idempotent membership, durable pending work, no permanent loss, explicit capacity, correct termination. | Note 30 |
| SEM-127 | Rejected universal | Idempotent visited insertion makes every BFS output retry-safe. | Parent appends and path-count addition are non-idempotent without set/contribution deduplication. | Replay algebra in note 30 |
| SEM-128 | Proved fact | A partial distributed checkpoint must include channel/in-flight effects or recreate them from a clean ancestor. | Otherwise a sent-but-not-recorded message can be lost from the recovered cut. | Chandy--Lamport model and note 30 |
| SEM-129 | Qualified fact | Replaying a level may choose a different parent while preserving one-arbitrary-shortest-parent correctness. | Parent edge/depth valid; deterministic/shortlex outputs require their full tie reduction. | Note 30 |
| SEM-130 | Rejected universal | Changing `hash(key) mod P` at restart safely repartitions visited state without migration. | Old/new owners can both or neither become authoritative for state and pending work. | Ownership-epoch argument in note 30 |
| SEM-131 | Proved fact | Durable termination requires the same global no-work/no-in-flight proof as live termination. | Consistent snapshot and complete output-specific obligations. | Note 30 and termination-detection sources |
| SEM-132 | Proved fact | An equal-depth edge plus two BFS-tree branches below their LCA is a replayable simple odd-cycle witness. | Finite simple undirected unweighted graph; exact BFS depths and parent tree. | Note 31 |
| SEM-133 | Rejected universal | Minimizing equal-depth conflict cycles from one arbitrary BFS root returns the graph's odd girth. | A six-vertex graph has odd girth 3 but the chosen root's only extracted conflict cycle has length 5. | Counterexample in note 31 |

| SEM-535 | Rejected universal | BFS depth on a condensation DAG is a unique topological rank and every arc advances one depth. | DAG arcs may go from a long-branch deep vertex to a target with a shorter independent source path. | Notes 41 and 84 |
| SEM-536 | Proved fact | For positive right Cayley alphabet `S`, SCCs are left cosets of subgroup `H=<S>_+ intersect <S>_+^-1`. | Exact group elements and positive-word reachability. | Algebraic derivation in note 84 |
| SEM-537 | Qualified fact | In a finite group, positive generator paths recover inverses, so SCCs are cosets of the subgroup generated by `S`; a generating `S` gives one SCC. | Finite group and right-action directed Cayley graph. | Finite-order proof in note 84 |
| SEM-538 | Rejected universal | A group-generating positive alphabet makes every infinite directed Cayley graph strongly connected. | `Z` with alphabet `{+1}` has singleton SCCs. | Note 84 |
| SEM-539 | Definition | The period of a finite strongly connected digraph is the GCD of its directed cycle lengths. | At least one arc/cycle; equivalent closed-walk definition. | Note 85 and irreducible-matrix theory |
| SEM-540 | Proved fact | All directed walks from one root to one vertex have lengths congruent modulo the graph period. | Finite strongly connected digraph. | Return-walk difference proof in note 85 |
| SEM-541 | Proved fact | Exact BFS depths recover period as `gcd_(u->v)(d(u)+1-d(v))`. | Complete distances and complete semantic arc coverage in one strongly connected digraph. | Two-divisibility proof in note 85 |
| SEM-542 | Proved calibration | On directed `C_N`, all depth slacks are zero except the closing arc slack `N`, so the period is `N`. | Standard one-way directed cycle. | Note 85 |
| SEM-543 | Proved fact | A connected undirected graph modeled with both arc directions has period two iff bipartite and period one otherwise. | Nontrivial connected graph; two-step backtracks included. | Even/odd GCD proof in note 85 |
| SEM-544 | Qualified fact | In a period-`p` strongly connected digraph, walks from `u` to `v` occur only in one length residue modulo `p`. | This residue restriction alone does not assert every small compatible length exists. | Notes 33 and 85 |
| SEM-545 | Proved fact | In a strongly connected Cayley digraph, positive word length modulo `p` defines a surjective homomorphism `chi:G->Z_p` sending every generator to one. | Exact group-element vertices and fixed positive right-action alphabet. | Note 85 |
| SEM-546 | Rejected universal | Strong connectivity implies directed aperiodicity. | Directed `C_N` is strongly connected with period `N`. | Notes 84 and 85 |
| SEM-547 | Qualified fact | A global GCD of local depth-slack GCDs certifies period only with finalized labels and complete arc coverage. | Correct duplicates are harmless; missing arcs can leave a falsely large multiple. | Notes 30, 51, and 85 |
| SEM-548 | Proved fact | For each ordered pair in a finite period-`p` strongly connected digraph, every sufficiently large walk length in its one allowed residue modulo `p` exists. | Finite graph; numerical semigroup of closed-walk lengths. | Note 86 |
| SEM-549 | Definition | A finite strongly connected aperiodic digraph is primitive; its exponent is the least `gamma` with fully positive Boolean support `A^gamma`. | Fixed adjacency and exact-length walk semantics. | Note 86 and primitive-matrix literature |
| SEM-550 | Qualified fact | Every `n`-vertex primitive digraph has exponent at most `(n-1)^2+1`. | Classical Wielandt bound; exponent is not BFS diameter. | Note 86; Wielandt |
| SEM-551 | Proved fact | A period greater than one prevents any adjacency power from having full all-pairs support. | Cyclic classes advance by walk length modulo `p`. | Notes 85 and 86 |
| SEM-552 | Rejected universal | Root BFS distances and graph period determine the primitive exponent. | Two three-vertex period-one digraphs share distances `(0,1,1)` but one has exponent one and the sparse one does not. | Note 86 |
| SEM-553 | Definition | `W_(k+1)=Post(W_k)` computes exact-length walk support, while BFS subtracts prior balls to retain only first discoveries. | Boolean unit-step transition semantics. | Notes 25, 33, and 86 |
| SEM-554 | Rejected universal | Permanent visited subtraction is valid for exact-length reachability. | Later revisits in the allowed residue are part of the requested support. | Note 86 |
| SEM-555 | Qualified fact | In an inverse-closed alphabet, any path can be padded immediately by even lengths, while period one guarantees all lengths only beyond a transient threshold. | Actual inverse steps are legal directed transitions. | Notes 16, 85, and 86 |
| SEM-556 | Scope boundary | Non-quiescent periodic support propagation is not a failed BFS termination detector when the declared object is exact-length walk support. | Finite strong component and `W_k` rather than first-discovery `F_k`. | Notes 18, 25, 30, and 86 |
| SEM-557 | Proved fact | Every shortest unit-edge walk is simple because any repeated-vertex segment can be deleted to shorten it. | Static directed or undirected unweighted graph. | Note 87 |
| SEM-558 | Qualified fact | Exact-length walk support can merge histories by endpoint, while exact-length simple-path legality generally cannot. | Walks allow revisits; simple paths forbid used vertices. | Notes 33, 86, and 87 |
| SEM-559 | Proved counterexample | Two equal-length arrivals at one vertex can have different legal simple-path continuations. | Five-vertex witness; only the history not already using `b` can continue through `b->t`. | Note 87 |
| SEM-560 | Definition | A direct exact simple-path state is `(v,U)` with used vertex set `U`; transition to `x` requires `x notin U`. | Vertex-simple path semantics. | Notes 20 and 87 |
| SEM-561 | Rejected universal | A global base-vertex visited set is exact for arbitrary exact-length simple-path queries. | It can merge the five-vertex witness histories and discard the only legal requested-length continuation. | Note 87 |
| SEM-562 | Scope boundary | General exact-length simple-path existence includes Hamiltonian Path at length `n-1` and is NP-complete when length is input. | General directed/undirected decision setting; fixed/parameterized `k` has specialized algorithms. | Note 87; Karp; color-coding literature |
| SEM-563 | Rejected universal | Nonbacktracking or freely reduced words guarantee a simple Cayley state path. | A longer relation can return to an earlier state without an immediate inverse pair. | Notes 39, 60, 66, and 87 |
| SEM-564 | Proved fact | Every Cayley geodesic word traces a simple state path. | Exact group-element state equality and positive unit generator costs. | Repeated-prefix deletion proof in note 87 |
| SEM-565 | Qualified fact | Endpoint-only owner routing may colocate simple-path histories but does not make them semantic duplicates. | Successor legality depends on the used set or other history state. | Notes 20, 51, and 87 |
| SEM-566 | Definition | A direct trail state is `(v,F)` with used semantic-edge set `F`; traversing edge `e` requires `e notin F`. | Fixed directed-arc or undirected-edge identity. | Note 88 |
| SEM-567 | Proved counterexample | Equal-length trails ending at one vertex can have different legal edge continuations. | Six-vertex witness; one history already used `v->x` and the other did not. | Note 88 |
| SEM-568 | Rejected universal | A global edge-used bitmap is exact across alternative trail histories. | One history's use of an edge must not ban it from a different history. | Notes 20 and 88 |
| SEM-569 | Proved fact | Nonempty directed trails in `D` correspond exactly to simple directed paths in the line digraph `L(D)`. | Directed arc identity; consecutive head-tail incidence. | Note 88; Harary-Norman |
| SEM-570 | Rejected universal | Every simple path in an ordinary undirected line graph lifts to a trail using the same original edges. | The three-edge star gives a three-vertex line path but no three-edge trail. | Note 88 |
| SEM-571 | Scope boundary | Eulerian trail existence has special degree/connectivity structure and does not validate endpoint-only dedup for generic constrained trails. | Declared directed or undirected Euler contract. | Note 88; Hierholzer |
| SEM-572 | Qualified fact | In an inverse-paired undirected Cayley model, `(g,s)` and `(gs,s^-1)` are two orientations of one trail edge; a directed model may count distinct arcs. | Explicit edge-identity and label convention. | Notes 06, 37, and 88 |
| SEM-573 | Proved fact | A finite strongly connected directed Cayley multigraph has an Euler circuit over labeled generator-arc occurrences. | One incoming/outgoing arc per vertex and generator; strong connectivity. | Balance argument in note 88 |
| SEM-574 | Qualified fact | Endpoint owner routing colocates trail histories but does not authorize merging different used-edge sets. | History-dependent edge legality. | Notes 51 and 88 |
| SEM-575 | Definition | A vertex dominates a target when every path from the root to that target contains it. | Directed flow graph; reachable target; reflexive convention stated separately. | Note 89; Lengauer-Tarjan |
| SEM-576 | Proved fact | If `d` dominates `v`, then `d` is an ancestor of `v` in every valid BFS parent tree. | Unit-edge BFS; parent chains are shortest root paths. | Direct implication in note 89 |
| SEM-577 | Rejected converse | An ancestor in one BFS parent tree need not dominate its descendant. | Parent ties may select one branch of a diamond. | Diamond counterexample in note 89 |
| SEM-578 | Proved fact | An immediate-dominator tree edge need not be a graph edge or join adjacent BFS layers. | Reachable flow graph. | Branch-and-rejoin witness in note 89; Lengauer-Tarjan |
| SEM-579 | Rejected equivalence | Mandatory on every shortest path is not equivalent to dominance on every path. | Longer bypasses remain in the original graph. | Unequal-length bypass counterexample in note 89 |
| SEM-580 | Qualified fact | The classical dominator equation intersects dominator sets over all reachable graph predecessors and requires fixed-point treatment on cycles. | Full flow graph, not only the shortest-path DAG. | Note 89; Cooper-Harvey-Kennedy |
| SEM-581 | Proved fact | For distinct root, candidate, and target, the candidate dominates the target iff deleting it makes the target unreachable from the root. | Declared graph and path semantics fixed. | Two-direction proof in note 89 |
| SEM-582 | Qualified fact | Distance equality plus exact prefix-count times suffix-count equaling the total certifies that a vertex lies on every shortest path. | Exact non-overflowing edge-path counts and matching reverse-graph semantics. | Decomposition proof in note 89; notes 30 and 41 |
| SEM-583 | Rejected universal | A complete BFS parent tree or shortest-path DAG suffices to establish full-graph dominance. | Non-shortest bypass arcs may avoid the candidate. | Note 89 |
| SEM-584 | Qualified fact | Missing or stale incoming arcs can create false dominators without changing observed reachability or shortest distance. | Incomplete distributed graph view or mixed epoch. | Longer-bypass witness and distributed analysis in note 89 |
| SEM-585 | Rejected universal | Strong connectivity or Cayley vertex transitivity eliminates nontrivial root-relative dominators. | Directed generator alphabets may provide only one forward cyclic route. | One-way directed-cycle Cayley witness in note 89 |
| SEM-586 | Classical theorem | In a finite digraph without a direct source-target arc, minimum internal vertex-separator size equals maximum internally vertex-disjoint directed path count. | Fixed distinct endpoints and declared internal-separator convention. | Note 90; Menger; Bang-Jensen-Gutin |
| SEM-587 | Proved equivalence | A non-endpoint vertex dominates the target iff its singleton set is an internal source-target separator. | Reachable target in the declared graph. | Notes 89 and 90 |
| SEM-588 | Qualified fact | For a reachable nonadjacent pair, a nontrivial dominator exists iff local internal vertex connectivity is one. | Finite directed graph; standard Menger conditions. | Note 90 |
| SEM-589 | Rejected equivalence | Two distinct paths or a shortest-path count of at least two proves two internally vertex-disjoint routes. | Distinct paths may merge at an unavoidable internal vertex. | Two-branch-then-join witness in note 90 |
| SEM-590 | Proved fact | Every intermediate BFS sphere separates the root from a target at greater distance and therefore upper-bounds their local vertex connectivity by its cardinality. | Exact directed unit-edge distances; intermediate layer excludes endpoints. | Notes 48 and 90; Menger |
| SEM-591 | Rejected equivalence | BFS frontier width equals minimum separator size or independent-route count. | Many branches may converge through one later dominator. | Arbitrarily wide branch-then-join family in note 90 |
| SEM-592 | Qualified fact | Applying Menger to the shortest-path DAG measures shortest-route separation, which may differ from full-graph separation. | Complete shortest-path DAG and explicit graph choice. | Unequal-length bypass witness in note 90 |
| SEM-593 | Scope boundary | Vertex-disjoint and edge/arc-disjoint connectivity answer different failure models, especially with shared states or parallel labeled arcs. | Explicit vertex and edge identity. | Note 90 |
| SEM-594 | Rejected universal | Strongly connected regular directed Cayley graphs necessarily have two internally vertex-disjoint routes between root and target. | Asymmetric generator directions may form a one-way cycle. | Directed-cycle witness in note 90 |
| SEM-595 | Proved example | An undirected cycle gives two internally vertex-disjoint routes between each nonadjacent pair. | Ordinary undirected cycle with at least four vertices. | Clockwise/counterclockwise construction in note 90 |
| SEM-596 | Rejected equivalence | Multiple owners, replicas, messages, or parent records constitute semantic path-disjointness evidence. | Distributed physical placement is separate from graph identity and incidence. | Note 90 |
| SEM-597 | Definition | `p` postdominates `v` when every path from `v` to the declared exit contains `p`. | Single-exit contract and exit-reachable vertex universe. | Note 91; Ferrante-Ottenstein-Warren |
| SEM-598 | Proved equivalence | Postdominance in a graph equals dominance rooted at the exit in the graph with every arc reversed. | Exact reverse transition relation. | Path-reversal proof in note 91 |
| SEM-599 | Rejected equivalence | A reverse BFS parent tree is a postdominator tree. | Reverse parents select one shortest suffix; alternative suffixes may exist. | Reversed-diamond witness in note 91 |
| SEM-600 | Rejected equivalence | Mandatory on every shortest suffix is equivalent to postdominance on every exit-reaching suffix. | Longer terminating bypasses may exist. | Unequal-length suffix witness in note 91 |
| SEM-601 | Rejected implication | Postdominance of the exit implies every execution eventually reaches the exit. | Infinite paths and dead-ending paths are outside ordinary exit-reaching-path quantification. | Self-loop plus exit witness in note 91 |
| SEM-602 | Qualified fact | With an absorbing target and adversarial choices in a finite graph, every maximal path reaches it iff no reachable avoiding cycle and no reachable non-target dead end exist. | Finite graph; all maximal paths; no fairness restriction suppressing choices. | Counterexecution argument in note 91 |
| SEM-603 | Definition | A virtual exit joined from all real exits makes postdominance quantify over every terminating path to any real exit. | Explicit multi-exit transformation. | Note 91 |
| SEM-604 | Scope boundary | Separate per-exit postdominance is conditional on that exit, while virtual-exit postdominance intersects obligations across all exits. | Multiple declared exits. | Note 91 |
| SEM-605 | Rejected equivalence | Reverse reachability, reverse distance, shortest-suffix gateways, postdominance, and inevitable target arrival are interchangeable backward-search results. | Their path quantifiers differ. | Contract ladder in note 91 |
| SEM-606 | Qualified fact | In asymmetric Cayley search, reverse BFS must use the declared reversed transition relation, not silently add algebraic inverse moves. | Directed generator alphabet. | Notes 41 and 91 |
| SEM-607 | Rejected implication | Distributed search quiescence proves that every semantic graph execution terminates. | Algorithm work termination and graph-path liveness are different properties. | Notes 56 and 91 |
| SEM-608 | Definition | Transitive closure and reduction preserve directed reachability, not unit-edge distance. | Fixed vertex universe and reachability relation. | Note 92; Aho-Garey-Ullman |
| SEM-609 | Rejected equivalence | Reachability-equivalent graphs have equivalent BFS layers, diameter, parents, or frontier widths. | Unit transitions may differ. | Chain-versus-closure family in note 92 |
| SEM-610 | Proved fact | Adding unit arcs cannot increase finite shortest distances; reachability-preserving deletion cannot decrease them. | Same vertices and unit costs. | Direct path-set inclusion proof in note 92 |
| SEM-611 | Rejected implication | Equal complete visited sets validate equal BFS computations. | Intermediate distances, layers, parents, path counts, and work can differ. | Note 92 |
| SEM-612 | Rejected invariant | Dominators, separators, and internally disjoint path counts are preserved by reachability equivalence. | A reachability-redundant shortcut can bypass old mandatory vertices. | Shortcut-chain witness in note 92 |
| SEM-613 | Proved fact | Exact reachability equivalence preserves SCC membership and the reachability order between SCCs. | Mutual directed reachability defines SCCs. | Note 92 |
| SEM-614 | Rejected invariant | Reachability-equivalent condensation DAG representations have equal BFS distances. | Reduction and closure of the same component order use different unit arcs. | Chain argument in note 92 |
| SEM-615 | Qualified fact | Adding a shortcut weighted by its exact old shortest distance preserves all old shortest distances. | Nonnegative weights and a valid old shortest-path witness. | Replacement argument in note 92 |
| SEM-616 | Proved bound | If `S` is contained in `T` and every `T` generator has `S`-word length at most `L`, then `d_T <= d_S <= L d_T`. | Cayley word metrics on the same group with finite symmetric alphabets. | Word-substitution proof in note 92 |
| SEM-617 | Rejected equivalence | Algebraically redundant generators are irrelevant to BFS. | They can shorten word distance, change degree, relations, frontiers, and duplicates. | Note 92 |
| SEM-618 | Scope boundary | Equality of groups generated using formal inverses need not imply equal positive-alphabet reachability in infinite groups or monoids. | Asymmetric directed generator contract. | Notes 06, 84, and 92 |
| SEM-619 | Rejected comparison | Multi-GPU timings remain implementation-only comparisons when the generator or shortcut graph changes. | Changed unit graph changes work, span, storage, and communication. | Note 92 |
| SEM-620 | Proved fact | Word metrics from two finite symmetric generating sets of one group are bi-Lipschitz equivalent via maximum generator-substitution lengths. | Both alphabets finite, symmetric, and generate the same group. | Substitution proof in note 93; de la Harpe |
| SEM-621 | Proved fact | The two BFS balls contain one another after linear radius rescaling by the substitution constants. | Same assumptions as the word-metric comparison. | Note 93 |
| SEM-622 | Qualified fact | Polynomial degree and exponential/subexponential/intermediate growth class are independent of the finite generating set. | Coarse ball-growth equivalence for a finitely generated group. | Note 93; de la Harpe; Loeh |
| SEM-623 | Rejected invariant | Exact sphere sizes, growth-series coefficients, girth, short relations, or parents are preserved under generator changes. | Quasi-isometry controls large scale, not exact integer layers. | `Z` alphabets with steps one versus one-and-two in note 93 |
| SEM-624 | Scope boundary | Existential quasi-isometry between individual finite puzzle graphs gives no uniform family-level diameter or BFS scaling bound. | Constants may grow with instance size. | Cyclic-family example in note 93 |
| SEM-625 | Qualified fact | Generator-substitution bounds transfer to Schreier orbit distances under the same action. | Each substituted word acts as the same group element on the declared state orbit. | Note 93 |
| SEM-626 | Rejected implication | Equality of groups generated with formal inverses supplies a directed positive-word metric comparison. | Positive semigroup reachability or bounded mutual positive simulation may fail. | Infinite-`Z` counterexample in note 93 |
| SEM-627 | Qualified fact | Finite groups recover inverse reachability through positive powers, but the required substitution length may be too large or nonuniform for scaling claims. | Finite group and generating alphabet. | Note 93 |
| SEM-628 | Scope boundary | Expanding a macro-generator geodesic gives a bounded replay-valid word, not necessarily a geodesic or canonical word in the base alphabet. | Correct stored substitutions and action orientation. | Note 93 |
| SEM-629 | Rejected implication | Bi-Lipschitz word-metric bounds predict BFS frontier peaks, candidate work, communication, or elapsed time. | Hardware costs depend on exact alphabet and layer geometry. | Note 93 |
| SEM-630 | Reporting rule | A generator-alphabet change is a graph/workload change in GPU or multi-GPU comparisons. | Exact unit-transition contract defines BFS work and depth. | Notes 68, 92, and 93 |
| SEM-631 | Proved fact | In an undirected Cayley graph, the next exact BFS frontier is the external vertex boundary of the completed ball. | Complete unit-edge BFS and exact state identity. | Notes 10, 46, and 94 |
| SEM-632 | Classical equivalence | A finitely generated group is amenable iff its Cayley graph admits finite nonempty sets with arbitrarily small boundary-to-volume ratio. | Finite symmetric generating set; specified boundary convention. | Note 94; Ceccherini-Silberstein-Grigorchuk-de la Harpe |
| SEM-633 | Rejected implication | Amenability alone makes the sequence of root-centered BFS balls a Følner sequence. | Følner's condition is existential over finite subsets, not specifically metric balls. | Note 94 |
| SEM-634 | Proved fact | A positive external-vertex isoperimetric constant forces every finite BFS ball's next frontier to be proportional to its visited volume. | Infinite locally finite graph and exact ball boundary. | Direct inequality in note 94 |
| SEM-635 | Proved consequence | Positive external-vertex isoperimetry implies at least exponential ball growth. | Uniform positive constant over all finite nonempty sets. | Ball recurrence in note 94 |
| SEM-636 | Rejected converse | Exponential group growth implies nonamenability or positive isoperimetric constant. | Amenable groups of exponential growth exist. | Note 94; lamplighter-class boundary |
| SEM-637 | Proved examples | Standard BFS balls in `Z` have vanishing next-frontier/ball ratio, while standard free-group balls retain a positive limiting ratio. | Standard symmetric generating sets. | Exact counts in note 94 |
| SEM-638 | Rejected implication | Amenability of an individual finite Cayley graph predicts its pre-saturation frontier profile. | The whole finite vertex set always has empty boundary. | Note 94 |
| SEM-639 | Qualified fact | Uniform expansion across a finite graph family can force wide pre-saturation frontiers despite eventual finite exhaustion. | Expansion bound restricted to relevant set sizes. | Notes 46 and 94 |
| SEM-640 | Rejected equivalence | Group amenability and amenability/boundary geometry of one Schreier action are interchangeable. | Stabilizers and orbit structure may change the action graph. | Note 94 |
| SEM-641 | Rejected implication | A small graph boundary-to-volume ratio guarantees low distributed communication. | Owner cut, routing, duplicates, and representation remain separate. | Notes 51 and 94 |
| SEM-642 | Rejected implication | Amenability or nonamenability predicts elapsed GPU BFS performance. | It constrains coarse semantic boundaries, not generator cost or hardware mechanisms. | Note 94 |
| SEM-643 | Definition | BFS distance, random-walk hitting time, mixing time, and cover time quantify different deterministic or probabilistic objects. | Declared graph and Markov transition rule. | Note 95 |
| SEM-644 | Qualified fact | A first-hit walk gives an upper-bound path witness after cycle deletion, not necessarily a shortest path. | Replay-valid trajectory in the declared graph. | Note 95 |
| SEM-645 | Proved example | On an `n`-vertex path from one endpoint to the other, BFS distance is `n-1` while simple-random-walk expected hitting time is `(n-1)^2`. | Ordinary undirected path walk. | Standard recurrence in note 95 |
| SEM-646 | Rejected implication | A long run with no newly visited states proves component exhaustion or unreachability of unseen states. | Finite random history without independent coverage certificate. | Note 95 |
| SEM-647 | Proved example | BFS discovers all of `K_n` at depth one while its simple-random-walk expected cover time is `(n-1)H_(n-1)`. | Complete undirected graph. | Coupon-collector calculation in note 95 |
| SEM-648 | Rejected implication | A mixed or stationary random walk has covered every state in its trajectory. | Marginal distribution and historical coverage are different events. | Complete-graph witness in note 95 |
| SEM-649 | Classical bound | Worst-case expected simple-random-walk cover time over connected `n`-vertex undirected graphs is cubic with tight asymptotic upper constant `4/27`. | Simple random walk; expectation maximized over starts. | Feige 1995; note 95 |
| SEM-650 | Qualified fact | A finite regular undirected Cayley walk has uniform stationary distribution, but may require laziness to remove periodicity. | Uniform generator-edge choice and connected graph. | Notes 33, 85, and 95 |
| SEM-651 | Rejected universal | `p` independent walkers divide hitting or cover time by `p`. | Overlap, bottlenecks, correlations through geometry, and rare states remain. | Note 95 |
| SEM-652 | Rejected implication | Exact deduplication of walker visits restores BFS distance order and finalization. | Coverage identity alone lacks minimum-depth scheduling. | Note 95 |
| SEM-653 | Qualified fact | A finite strongly connected directed Cayley multigraph under uniform labeled-generator choice has uniform stationary distribution. | Balanced labeled in/out multiplicity; irreducible chain. | Notes 84, 88, and 95 |
| SEM-654 | Rejected implication | High GPU random-walk step throughput implies high unique coverage, short hitting time, or exact BFS progress. | Step multiplicity and target/state coverage differ. | Note 95 |
| SEM-655 | Proved fact | Under reliable synchronous all-neighbor flooding with unit edge delay, a vertex's first-receipt round equals its BFS distance from the source. | Fixed graph, complete forwarding, one-round delivery. | Two-sided path proof in note 96 |
| SEM-656 | Proved fact | The newly informed set in each exact flooding round equals the corresponding BFS sphere. | Same exact flooding contract. | Note 96 |
| SEM-657 | Qualified fact | Forward-once flooding uses at most one message per directed edge orientation and a first sender can define a BFS parent. | Undirected graph, reliable rounds, duplicate message identity. | Note 96 |
| SEM-658 | Proved fact | Exact single-source flooding broadcast time equals source eccentricity, not automatically graph diameter. | Complete reachable-component delivery. | Notes 21 and 96 |
| SEM-659 | Qualified fact | Immediate forwarding with fixed nonnegative edge latencies computes earliest arrival in the weighted path metric rather than hop distance. | Static latencies and no queue/history effects. | Note 96 |
| SEM-660 | Rejected implication | One-shot flooding remains complete when transmissions may be lost. | The only copy across a cut may disappear. | Note 96 |
| SEM-661 | Rejected equivalence | Randomized rumor-spreading receipt rounds are BFS distances in the underlying communication graph. | Sparse random contacts define protocol time, not all-edge hop layers. | Note 96; Karp et al. |
| SEM-662 | Rejected implication | First receipt safely finalizes minimum distance under arbitrary asynchronous delivery. | Later shorter proposals may arrive unless scheduling/finalization is distance-safe. | Notes 18 and 96 |
| SEM-663 | Rejected implication | Local silence or empty local queues prove global flooding/BFS completion. | Unknown vertices and in-flight messages may remain. | Notes 56 and 96 |
| SEM-664 | Qualified fact | Bulk-synchronous multi-GPU BFS preserves flooding layers only if every level's remote discoveries enter the next logical level exactly. | Lossless routing, exact identity, completed barrier or equivalent protocol. | Notes 07, 51, and 96 |
| SEM-665 | Rejected implication | Coalescing duplicate messages permits dropping every copy of one semantic discovery. | At least one exact authoritative insertion must survive. | Note 96 |
| SEM-666 | Reporting rule | Sampling a subset of generators or peers is random/partial exploration unless that sampled temporal graph is the declared problem. | Original exact Cayley BFS requires complete successor semantics. | Note 96 |
| SEM-667 | Definition | An end of a connected locally finite undirected graph is an equivalence class of rays whose tails cannot be separated by any finite vertex set. | Infinite undirected graph. | Note 97; Diestel |
| SEM-668 | Proved fact | The number of infinite components outside nested finite BFS balls is nondecreasing with radius. | Connected locally finite graph. | Finite-shell splitting argument in note 97 |
| SEM-669 | Qualified fact | If a graph has finitely many ends, sufficiently large BFS balls separate them into that many infinite outside components. | Connected locally finite graph. | Note 97; end definition |
| SEM-670 | Proved bound | The number of infinite components outside `B_r` is at most the size of `S_(r+1)`. | Exact BFS ball in a connected locally finite graph. | Boundary-incidence proof in note 97 |
| SEM-671 | Rejected implication | A growing or large BFS frontier implies multiple graph ends. | Many sphere vertices may reconnect in one outside component. | `Z^2` one-ended example in note 97 |
| SEM-672 | Proved examples | A ray has one end, `Z` has two, `Z^2` has one, and a regular tree of degree at least three has infinitely many. | Standard undirected graphs/generators. | Note 97 |
| SEM-673 | Rejected implication | Any bounded exact BFS prefix determines the graph's number of ends. | Identical prefixes can continue as a ray, branching tree, or finite cycle. | Continuation counterexamples in note 97 |
| SEM-674 | Classical theorem | A finitely generated group has zero, one, two, or infinitely many ends. | Ends of a finite-generator Cayley graph. | Freudenthal-Hopf; Möller survey; note 97 |
| SEM-675 | Qualified fact | The number of group ends is independent of the finite symmetric generating set. | Finitely generated group and quasi-isometric Cayley graphs. | Notes 93 and 97 |
| SEM-676 | Rejected invariant | Exact BFS spheres and the radius where ends visibly separate are generator-independent. | Quasi-isometry preserves ends but can rescale and reshape balls. | Note 97 |
| SEM-677 | Rejected implication | Cayley-group end count transfers automatically to every Schreier graph or finite orbit. | Stabilizers and quotient connectivity can change the end space. | Note 97 |
| SEM-678 | Rejected implication | Graph end count predicts BFS peak memory, duplicate work, owner balance, or communication. | Persistent directions and physical workload/partition geometry are different objects. | Note 97 |
| SEM-679 | Definition | Independent bond percolation samples each semantic edge open with probability `p`; conditional on that realization, BFS is ordinary exact BFS on the open subgraph. | Fixed edge-identity and one quenched realization. | Note 98; Grimmett |
| SEM-680 | Rejected equivalence | A quenched frontier/cluster and its annealed expectation or survival probability are interchangeable evidence. | Expectations average over distinct realized graphs. | Note 98 |
| SEM-681 | Proved fact | On a bond-percolated `q`-regular tree, `E|F_r| = q p ((q-1)p)^(r-1)` for `r>=1`. | Independent edge states and root at the tree origin. | Branching-process calculation in note 98 |
| SEM-682 | Classical fact | Bond percolation on a `q`-regular tree has critical probability `1/(q-1)`. | Infinite tree, `q>=3`. | Grimmett; note 98 |
| SEM-683 | Rejected implication | Positive or constant expected frontier size proves positive survival probability for a realized cluster. | At criticality expected generations stay positive while extinction is almost sure. | Tree counterexample in note 98 |
| SEM-684 | Rejected implication | Expected branching factor above one guarantees every trial survives or proves a giant component on a general graph. | Early extinction, collisions, dependence, bottlenecks, and finite scaling remain. | Note 98 |
| SEM-685 | Rejected equivalence | Infinite cluster, finite-family giant component, and survival through a tested radius are the same event. | They use different graph limits and quantifiers. | Note 98 |
| SEM-686 | Qualified fact | Cayley transitivity makes the root-cluster distribution root-independent under invariant percolation, but not each sampled cluster vertex-transitive. | Homogeneous independent percolation on a transitive graph. | Note 98; Benjamini-Schramm |
| SEM-687 | Rejected invariant | Percolation thresholds and random-frontier laws depend only on the generated group, not the generator graph. | Generator changes alter degree, cycles, and edge substrate. | Notes 92, 93, and 98 |
| SEM-688 | Rejected equivalence | Static closed edges and transient independently lost messages represent the same random graph process. | Retry changes transient loss but not quenched bond state. | Notes 96 and 98 |
| SEM-689 | Reporting rule | Randomness must be keyed explicitly to semantic undirected edges, directed arcs, labels, trials, or transmission attempts. | These identities define different stochastic graphs. | Note 98 |
| SEM-690 | Rejected equivalence | Capacity overflow or dropped distributed work may be counted as a percolation-closed transition. | Model randomness and execution failure are distinct. | Notes 55 and 98 |
| SEM-691 | Qualified fact | Mean frontier or peak alone is insufficient for stochastic GPU capacity planning; realization tails and explicit failure accounting matter. | Declared trial distribution and capacity contract. | Note 98 |
| SEM-692 | Definition | Regularity, coverage, uniqueness, geodesicity, and prefix closure are separate properties of a generator-word language. | Fixed alphabet, evaluation map, and word metric. | Note 99 |
| SEM-693 | Proved fact | A covering, unique, geodesic language has accepted length-`r` words in bijection with the radius-`r` Cayley BFS sphere. | Group Cayley graph and declared generator metric. | Direct bijection in note 99 |
| SEM-694 | Qualified fact | Prefix closure upgrades a unique geodesic language to a canonical rooted geodesic prefix tree. | Every accepted prefix remains accepted. | Note 99 |
| SEM-695 | Proved fact | Length counts of a finite automaton satisfy `a_r=u^T M^r v` and have a rational generating series. | Finite transition-count matrix. | Matrix calculation in note 99 |
| SEM-696 | Rejected equivalence | The rational accepted-word series of a DFA is automatically the spherical growth series of the evaluated Cayley graph. | Equality additionally needs coverage, uniqueness, and geodesicity. | Note 99 |
| SEM-697 | Rejected implication | A regular unique normal-form language is necessarily geodesic. | Padded unique representatives of `Z` preserve identity while inflating length. | Integer counterexample in note 99 |
| SEM-698 | Rejected implication | A regular geodesic language necessarily represents every state uniquely. | Distinct parallel labels can give distinct shortest words for one state. | Labeled integer-line counterexample in note 99 |
| SEM-699 | Rejected implication | A freely reduced generator word is geodesic in the quotient group. | Quotient relations can shorten words without adjacent inverse cancellation. | `C_3` counterexample in note 99 |
| SEM-700 | Qualified fact | An automatic structure need not use unique or geodesic representatives unless the chosen structure explicitly has those properties. | Standard automatic-group language and fellow-traveler contract. | Epstein et al.; note 99 |
| SEM-701 | Reporting rule | Per-instance regularity of a finite chosen normal-form set is no scalability result without family-level automaton size and construction evidence. | Finite state spaces across an intended family. | Note 99 |
| SEM-702 | Rejected implication | Uniqueness of group normal forms implies uniqueness of puzzle-state representatives in a Schreier action. | Stabilizer elements collapse distinct group elements into one orbit state. | Notes 16, 61, 62, and 99 |
| SEM-703 | Qualified fact | A normal-form automaton can replace state `visited` only after state-level coverage, uniqueness, and geodesicity are proved. | Actual evaluated group or orbit state contract. | Note 99 |
| SEM-704 | Reporting rule | A regular or canonical word language alone implies no single-GPU or multi-GPU performance gain. | Explicit automaton, state identity, family, workload, and measurements required. | Note 99 |
| SEM-705 | Definition | A Dinitz level graph retains positive-residual arcs `u->v` with `ell(v)=ell(u)+1` for exact residual BFS labels. | One coherent residual snapshot. | Dinitz; note 100 |
| SEM-706 | Proved fact | The `s-t` paths in a residual level graph are exactly the current shortest residual `s-t` paths. | Reachable sink and exact unit-arc BFS levels. | Level-telescoping proof in note 100 |
| SEM-707 | Definition | A blocking flow leaves no residual `s-t` path in the current level graph; it need not maximize flow inside that graph. | Declared residual capacities and level graph. | Dinitz; note 100 |
| SEM-708 | Proved fact | After a blocking-flow phase, the next reachable residual `s-t` distance is strictly greater than its old value. | Exact old levels and all old level paths blocked. | Old-level potential proof in note 100 |
| SEM-709 | Qualified fact | Failure of residual BFS to reach `t` certifies absence of an augmenting path and, with max-flow/min-cut theory, optimality of the current feasible flow. | Coherent exact residual graph. | Note 100 |
| SEM-710 | Qualified fact | Hopcroft-Karp BFS is multi-source/multi-target BFS in an alternating graph determined by the current matching. | Bipartite graph and current matching. | Hopcroft-Karp; note 100 |
| SEM-711 | Classical fact | Augmenting along a maximal family of vertex-disjoint shortest augmenting paths strictly increases the next augmenting-path length. | Hopcroft-Karp phase contract. | Hopcroft-Karp; note 100 |
| SEM-712 | Classical fact | A matching is maximum exactly when no augmenting path exists. | Finite graph and valid matching. | Berge's lemma; note 100 |
| SEM-713 | Rejected implication | One shortest augmentation necessarily exhausts the current shortest-distance phase. | Independent tied shortest routes may survive. | Two-path counterexample in note 100 |
| SEM-714 | Rejected invariant | BFS levels from one residual or alternating snapshot remain valid after arbitrary augmentation. | Augmentation changes capacities, reverse arcs, or matching orientation. | Note 100 |
| SEM-715 | Rejected equivalence | First discovery of a target completes construction of every tied shortest improvement route. | Partial frontier stopping can omit other shortest routes. | Note 100 |
| SEM-716 | Qualified fact | Phase-local dead-branch marks are not permanent reachability facts across residual mutations. | Graph is rebuilt between phases. | Note 100 |
| SEM-717 | Reporting rule | Frontier width, retained level edges, augmentation count, and objective gain are distinct phase measurements. | Explicit phase and objective contract. | Note 100 |
| SEM-718 | Reporting rule | Fast GPU level construction alone is not end-to-end flow or matching evidence. | Mutation, conflicts, blocking/disjointness, synchronization, and gain must also be measured. | Note 100 |
| SEM-719 | Representation equivalence | A root distance map and the indexed exact layer sets determine each other; replacing layer sets by their cardinalities loses vertex identity. | Layer indices and vertex identities retained; connected rooted graph. | Corrected note 101; audit E05 |
| SEM-720 | Proved fact | Rooted graph isomorphism preserves every BFS layer and hence the complete layer-size profile. | Root-preserving graph isomorphism. | Path-length preservation proof in note 101 |
| SEM-721 | Rejected converse | Equal complete rooted BFS profiles imply rooted or unrooted graph isomorphism. | Triangular prism and `K_(3,3)` both have profile `[1,3,2]`. | Note 101 |

| SEM-723 | Rejected implication | An identical bounded BFS neighborhood certifies equal graph order, diameter, or global structure. | Sufficiently long cycles of different lengths agree through any fixed radius. | Note 101 |
| SEM-724 | Proved fact | A complete all-pairs distance matrix determines a connected simple unweighted graph up to simultaneous vertex permutation. | Adjacency is exactly distance one. | Note 101 |
| SEM-725 | Qualified fact | Root-individualized color refinement can split vertices within one BFS layer using neighbor-color multisets. | Declared initial coloring and refinement rule. | Prism witness in note 101 |
| SEM-726 | Rejected equivalence | Color refinement and rooted BFS compute the same partition. | Refinement can use lateral and cross-layer neighbor multiplicities. | Note 101 |
| SEM-727 | Rejected universal | Uniform 1-WL distinguishes all nonisomorphic graphs. | Same-order regular graphs retain one uniform color class. | Prism versus `K_(3,3)`; Grohe |
| SEM-728 | Proved fact | Every root of a Cayley graph has the same BFS profile by translation symmetry. | Fixed Cayley graph and action convention. | Note 101 |
| SEM-729 | Rejected implication | Equal BFS profiles provide exact state identity in a Cayley or transitive graph. | Symmetry forces equality for distinct vertices. | Notes 28 and 101 |
| SEM-730 | Qualified fact | A profile mismatch can refute equivalence to a reference, while a profile match cannot prove exact frontier-set equality. | Same graph contract, root, and completed depths. | Note 101 |
| SEM-731 | Reporting rule | One-root, all-root, and sampled-root profile evidence have different quantifiers and must be labeled. | Declared root selection and traversal completeness. | Note 101 |
| SEM-732 | Reporting rule | Cheap multi-GPU reductions of layer statistics are aggregate checks, not deterministic distributed state-identity evidence. | Explicit digest/key and collision contract. | Notes 28, 37, and 101 |
| SEM-733 | Proved fact | After `r` strictly local message-passing rounds, a vertex representation depends only on its radius-`r` attributed neighborhood. | Shared local functions and no global information channel. | Induction in note 102 |
| SEM-734 | Rejected implication | Radius-`r` receptive field means the embedding faithfully retains every vertex, identity, or distance in that ball. | Aggregation may be many-to-one and fixed-width. | Note 102 |
| SEM-735 | Proved fact | Exact synchronous `min` relaxation from a marked source yields exact distances at most `r` after `r` unit-edge rounds. | Exact arithmetic, completed rounds, and fixed graph. | Bounded-walk induction in note 102 |
| SEM-736 | Proved fact | Exact Boolean neighborhood propagation reproduces BFS balls and frontiers by `B_(r+1)=B_r union N(B_r)`. | Exact state sets and completed synchronous rounds. | Notes 25 and 102 |
| SEM-737 | Rejected implication | Homogeneous local message passing on a symmetric regular graph spontaneously recovers source-relative BFS layers. | No source marker breaks translation/vertex symmetry. | Note 102 |
| SEM-738 | Qualified fact | Standard neighborhood-aggregation MPNNs are at most as graph-distinguishing as 1-WL; injective designs can match that bound. | Standard permutation-invariant local architecture assumptions. | Xu et al.; Morris et al.; note 102 |
| SEM-739 | Scope boundary | Global attention, higher-order tuple states, unique IDs, positional encodings, or supplied distances fall outside the plain local-MPNN/1-WL statement. | Architecture and inputs explicitly declared. | Note 102 |
| SEM-740 | Rejected equivalence | Repeated sum/message aggregation computes exact first-arrival BFS spheres. | It naturally accumulates walk multiplicity without `visited` subtraction. | Notes 33 and 102 |
| SEM-741 | Qualified fact | Rapid ball growth and narrow information funnels can force many dependencies into fixed-width embeddings, producing over-squashing. | Task needs long-range information and declared message-passing architecture. | Alon-Yahav; note 102 |
| SEM-742 | Rejected universal | Every Cayley BFS ball grows exponentially and therefore has the same over-squashing geometry. | Relations, polynomial growth, and finite saturation change ball growth. | Notes 10, 93, and 102 |
| SEM-743 | Rejected implication | Equal learned embeddings imply exact equality of Cayley or puzzle states. | Model representations are not proved injective state encodings. | Notes 28 and 102 |

| SEM-745 | Qualified fact | Generator labels preserve move type but do not identify a Cayley vertex under translation symmetry. | Standard labeled Cayley convention. | Note 102 |
| SEM-746 | Reporting rule | Message-passing throughput and exact unique-state BFS throughput are different GPU measurements. | Materialization, feature width, identity, aggregation, and output contract declared. | Note 102 |
| SEM-747 | Definition | Compact description length, state-encoding length, expanded vertices, and expanded arcs are distinct complexity parameters. | Declared implicit representation. | Note 103 |
| SEM-748 | Proved fact | A polynomial-size circuit over two `n`-bit vertex names can describe a directed graph with `2^n` vertices. | Circuit-succinct adjacency representation. | Galperin-Wigderson; note 103 |
| SEM-749 | Rejected implication | Compact adjacency membership automatically gives cheap complete successor enumeration. | A circuit may require testing exponentially many destination names. | Note 103 |
| SEM-750 | Qualified fact | A bounded named-move oracle avoids universe-wide adjacency scans but does not bound reachable volume or depth polynomially in state bits. | Exact direct successor access. | Note 103 |
| SEM-751 | Classical fact | Directed reachability in circuit-succinct graphs is PSPACE-complete by compact-description size. | Standard succinct representation and binary source/target names. | Configuration-graph hardness and Savitch upper bound; note 103 |

| SEM-754 | Output lower bound | Explicitly listing an exponential reached component, distance table, or parent tree requires exponential output volume. | Output names every reached state or record. | Note 103 |
| SEM-755 | Rejected equivalence | A compact scalar shortest distance and a replayable shortest path have the same output size. | A path may be exponentially long while its length needs `O(n)` bits. | Note 103 |
| SEM-756 | Rejected universal | Symbolic frontier or visited representations remain polynomially compact for every implicit graph. | Exact symbolic sets can have exponential representation/operation cost. | Note 103 |
| SEM-757 | Qualified fact | Special group/action structure may answer some reachability or membership queries without enumerating the whole Cayley state graph. | Problem-specific algebra and output contract. | Note 103 |
| SEM-758 | Proved capacity identity | Multiplying exact-visited memory by `p` raises a worst-case `2^n` state-universe limit by at most `log_2(p)` state bits at fixed bits per state. | Full universe occupancy and fixed representation cost. | Algebra in note 103 |
| SEM-759 | Rejected implication | Linear multi-GPU capacity scaling removes exponential state-space growth. | Device count enters only logarithmically in the feasible state-bit parameter under the full-universe model. | Note 103 |
| SEM-760 | Reporting rule | Succinct-BFS evidence must state whether complexity is normalized by compact input, valid universe, reachable graph, generated moves, or explicit output. | Declared access and output model. | Note 103 |
| SEM-761 | Definition | Multi-source BFS depth on a DAG is the minimum source-to-vertex path length, while synchronous Kahn wave rank is the maximum. | Finite DAG and all indegree-zero vertices as sources. | Note 104 |
| SEM-762 | Proved fact | A vertex appears in Kahn wave `1+max` of its predecessor waves. | Synchronous removal of each complete zero-indegree wave. | Induction in note 104 |
| SEM-763 | Proved fact | The Kahn wave rank equals longest directed path length from a source. | Finite DAG. | Induction in note 104 |
| SEM-764 | Proved fact | On the same DAG and source set, BFS depth never exceeds Kahn wave rank. | Every vertex is reachable from some DAG source. | Minimum-versus-maximum path lengths; note 104 |
| SEM-765 | Rejected equivalence | BFS layers and topological readiness waves are identical on every DAG. | `s->v` and `s->u->v` give depths one and two for `v`. | Note 104 |
| SEM-766 | Qualified fact | BFS and Kahn ranks coincide everywhere on a source-graded DAG whose every arc advances rank by one. | Finite graded DAG. | Note 104 |
| SEM-767 | Rejected equivalence | A Kahn wave partition is one unique topological order. | Vertices within a wave admit arbitrary valid tie orders. | Note 104 |
| SEM-768 | Rejected implication | Sorting an arbitrary DAG by multi-source BFS depth always gives a topological order. | An arc may enter a vertex having a shorter alternate source path. | Notes 84 and 104 |
| SEM-769 | Qualified fact | Nonempty residual vertices after exhaustive Kahn removal certify a directed cycle. | Procedure initialized over the complete finite graph with exact indegrees. | Kahn; note 104 |
| SEM-770 | Rejected equivalence | Empty BFS frontier and failed Kahn progress are the same certificate. | BFS exhausts a reachable set; Kahn residual certifies cyclic dependency globally. | Note 104 |
| SEM-771 | Proved fact | Unit-task Kahn height is longest dependency-path length plus one; weighted earliest completion uses a max-plus recurrence. | Unlimited parallel resources and precedence-only lower bound. | Note 104 |
| SEM-772 | Rejected equivalence | Critical dependency path is a weighted shortest path or BFS distance. | Dependencies require all predecessors, hence `max`, not `min`. | Note 104 |

| SEM-774 | Reporting rule | BFS candidate transitions, Kahn dependency retirements, completed tasks, and critical-path progress are different GPU work measures. | Declared algorithm and synchronization contract. | Note 104 |

| SEM-781 | Rejected implication | Refining a four-neighbor grid alone makes its normalized BFS distance converge to Euclidean distance. | The local stencil continues to induce `L1`. | Note 105 |

| SEM-783 | Qualified fact | A scalar multi-source distance transform does not encode the unique nearest source; labels and ties require extra output. | Source identities retained under declared tie semantics. | Notes 13 and 105 |

| SEM-789 | Proved fact | Complete undirected component BFS and unioning every graph edge produce the same connected-component partition. | Finite graph, exact vertices, and complete edge processing. | Note 106 |
| SEM-790 | Rejected equivalence | A disjoint-set representative forest is a BFS or replayable graph-path forest. | Union roots and path-compression pointers need not be graph edges. | Note 106 |

| SEM-793 | Rejected capability | Plain union-find can undo an arbitrary edge deletion and split exactly when a bridge was removed. | Compressed set state omits alternative graph-edge structure. | Note 106 |
| SEM-794 | Rejected equivalence | Unioning endpoints of directed arcs computes directed reachability or SCCs. | It computes weak connectivity after discarding orientation. | One-arc witness in note 106 |

| SEM-796 | Reporting rule | DSU inequality proves separation only relative to the complete set of edges processed so far. | Edge-stream completeness explicitly scoped. | Note 106 |
| SEM-797 | Rejected implication | Union-find over discovered implicit edges certifies that no unseen legal move can connect two sets. | DSU does not enumerate successors. | Note 106 |
| SEM-798 | Qualified fact | Fully enumerated inverse-closed Cayley/Schreier edges can yield orbit components through DSU but not minimum word lengths. | Finite exact state enumeration and undirected move contract. | Note 106 |
