| SEM-800 | Rejected equivalence | Parallel union/hooking/compression rounds are BFS distance levels. | Connectivity algorithms need not propagate from one root by metric frontier. | Shiloach-Vishkin; note 106 |
| SEM-801 | Rejected implication | Path compression validates semantic state identity or hash collision safety. | DSU assumes exact element names before union operations. | Notes 28 and 106 |
| SEM-802 | Reporting rule | Component-label throughput and shortest-path BFS transition/frontier throughput are different GPU claims. | Declared output and update model. | Note 106 |
| SEM-803 | Definition | A BFS/SPT preserves every root distance, while an MST minimizes the root-free sum of selected edge weights. | Connected undirected graph with declared weights. | Note 107 |
| SEM-804 | Proved fact | With all edge weights one, every spanning tree is an MST because each has `|V|-1` edges. | Connected finite graph. | Note 107 |
| SEM-805 | Rejected implication | An unweighted MST is necessarily a BFS tree from a chosen root. | A path spanning tree of `K_n` is an MST but has depth `n-1` instead of one. | Note 107 |
| SEM-806 | Qualified fact | Every unweighted BFS tree is an MST, but only because all spanning trees tie on total unit weight. | Connected unit-weight graph. | Note 107 |
| SEM-807 | Rejected implication | A weighted MST is necessarily an SPT from a chosen root. | Three-vertex weighted counterexample. | Note 107 |
| SEM-808 | Rejected implication | A weighted SPT necessarily has minimum total spanning-tree weight. | The same triangle's star SPT weighs more than its MST. | Note 107 |
| SEM-809 | Rejected universal | MST root-distance stretch or SPT total-weight overhead is bounded by a graph-independent constant. | Root-star plus slightly cheaper leaf chain gives growing separation. | Note 107 |
| SEM-810 | Classical fact | The path between two vertices in an MST minimizes the maximum edge weight among all connecting paths. | Connected undirected weighted graph. | Cut/exchange proof in note 107 |
| SEM-811 | Rejected equivalence | MST bottleneck-optimal paths minimize additive weight or hop count. | Maximum-edge and sum/length objectives differ. | Note 107 |
| SEM-812 | Qualified fact | Distinct edge weights imply a unique MST but do not imply that tree preserves root shortest paths. | Connected weighted graph. | Note 107 |
| SEM-813 | Reporting rule | BFS/SPT distance-label certificates and MST cut/cycle certificates validate different objectives. | Declared tree and graph weights. | Note 107 |
| SEM-814 | Qualified fact | In a finite unit-generator Cayley/Schreier graph every spanning tree is an MST, while only BFS trees select geodesic root normal forms. | Connected finite unit-weight graph. | Note 107 |
| SEM-815 | Rejected implication | A weighted-generator Cayley MST preserves minimum weighted word cost from the identity. | Global tree weight and rooted word distance differ. | Note 107 |
| SEM-816 | Reporting rule | Shared edge-processing primitives do not make MST throughput evidence into BFS throughput evidence. | Surrounding ordering, synchronization, and output contract declared. | Note 107 |

| SEM-818 | Proved fact | On a unit-resistance tree, effective resistance equals graph distance. | Unique path and series law. | Note 108 |

| SEM-820 | Rejected implication | Small effective resistance implies small BFS distance. | `k` parallel length-`L` paths have distance `L` and resistance `L/k`. | Note 108 |

| SEM-822 | Proved example | Distinct vertices of `K_n` have BFS distance one and effective resistance `2/n`. | Unit-resistance complete graph. | Symmetry calculation in note 108 |
| SEM-823 | Rejected implication | A complete BFS layer-size profile determines pairwise effective resistance. | Profiles omit edge arrangement and parallel route structure. | Notes 32, 101, and 108 |

| SEM-825 | Rejected equivalence | Effective resistance is the minimum additive edge resistance of one path. | Parallel paths carry current jointly. | Note 108 |
| SEM-826 | Qualified fact | The Laplacian-pseudoinverse quadratic form gives effective resistance. | Connected weighted undirected graph with positive conductances. | Note 108 |
| SEM-827 | Qualified fact | Cayley translation reduces pair resistance to identity-relative resistance, as it does word distance. | Finite undirected labeled Cayley network with invariant edge weights. | Note 108 |
| SEM-828 | Proof boundary | Cayley vertex transitivity alone does not prove effective resistance depends only on BFS word length. | Root-fixing sphere transitivity or direct resistance evidence is additionally needed. | Note 108 |
| SEM-829 | Scope boundary | Standard symmetric effective-resistance results do not transfer automatically to directed positive-alphabet reachability. | Directed resistance needs a separately declared definition. | Note 108 |
| SEM-830 | Reporting rule | Laplacian-solver throughput/tolerance and exact BFS frontier/identity throughput are different GPU claims. | Declared output, precision, and stopping contract. | Note 108 |

| SEM-832 | Proved fact | Every tree is zero-hyperbolic. | Geodesic tree metric. | Four-point/tree-tripod argument; note 109 |

| SEM-834 | Rejected implication | One BFS distance profile determines exact graph hyperbolicity. | Arbitrary quadruples require pair distances absent from one row. | Note 109 |

| SEM-837 | Rejected implication | Equal hyperbolicity implies comparable BFS growth. | Paths and regular trees are both zero-hyperbolic with radically different sphere growth. | Note 109 |
| SEM-838 | Rejected implication | Girth alone controls hyperbolicity. | Large grids retain girth four with growing hyperbolicity; long cycles have growing girth and hyperbolicity. | Note 109 |
| SEM-839 | Scope boundary | Every finite graph has a finite diameter-scale hyperbolicity constant, so an unquantified finite-instance claim is weak. | Finite connected graph. | Note 109 |
| SEM-840 | Proved fact | `d(s,x)+d(x,t)=d(s,t)` exactly characterizes membership of `x` on some shortest `s-t` path. | Any graph metric. | Triangle inequality and geodesic concatenation; note 109 |
| SEM-841 | Qualified fact | Thinness constrains geodesic corridors but not their vertex count, shortest-path multiplicity, or BFS frontier width. | Additional theorems needed for algorithmic pruning. | Note 109 |
| SEM-842 | Classical fact | Word-hyperbolicity is independent of the chosen finite generating set, while numerical `delta` and BFS spheres are generator-dependent. | Finitely generated group and finite symmetric generating sets. | Gromov; note 109 |

| SEM-844 | Evidence boundary | Sampled four-point gaps provide witnessed lower bounds, not exact hyperbolicity or upper-bound certificates. | No proved covering argument. | Four-point definition; note 109 |
| SEM-845 | Reporting rule | Hyperbolicity evidence and distance-oracle work must remain separate from frontier, visited, duplicate, balance, and communication performance claims. | GPU and multi-GPU studies. | Note 109 |
| SEM-846 | Definition | Exact BFS through radius `r` constructs the metric ball `B(c,r)`, while a graph interval collects vertices on shortest paths between two endpoints. | Connected undirected unweighted graph. | Note 110 |
| SEM-847 | Rejected implication | Every BFS ball is geodesically convex. | The radius-two ball in `C6` contains vertices two and four but excludes their unique shortest-path midpoint three. | Note 110 |
| SEM-848 | Classical fact | Every gated set is convex and each outside vertex has a unique gate satisfying one projection equality for all destinations in the set. | Standard graph metric. | Bandelt-Chepoi; note 110 |
| SEM-849 | Classical fact | Finite pairwise-intersecting families of gated sets have nonempty total intersection. | Gated subsets of a graph metric. | Bandelt-Chepoi; note 110 |
| SEM-850 | Definition | A Helly graph is one whose finite pairwise-intersecting metric-ball families have nonempty total intersection. | Undirected graph metric. | Bandelt-Chepoi; Ducoffe; note 110 |
| SEM-851 | Proved fact | Two integer-radius graph balls intersect exactly when the center distance is at most the sum of radii. | Connected unweighted graph. | Shortest-path construction; note 110 |
| SEM-852 | Rejected implication | Pairwise-intersecting balls always have a common vertex. | Four radius-one balls in `C4` pairwise intersect but have empty total intersection. | Note 110 |
| SEM-853 | Proved fact | Every finite Helly graph has `radius=ceil(diameter/2)`. | Ball-Helly property and finite connected graph. | Pairwise radius-ball proof; note 110 |
| SEM-854 | Rejected implication | The Helly radius identity makes ordinary two-sweep BFS an exact diameter/center algorithm. | The identity is existential and does not validate a sweep selection rule. | REF-021 and note 110 |
| SEM-855 | Semantic fact | An intersection of balls represents simultaneous distance upper bounds; their union represents satisfying at least one bound. | Fixed graph metric and centers/radii. | Note 110 |
| SEM-856 | Qualified fact | Pairwise distance checks certify joint ball feasibility only after the graph's Helly property is established. | Exact distances and ball-Helly graph. | Note 110 |
| SEM-857 | Rejected implication | Helly graphs have uniformly bounded hyperbolicity. | Strong square grids are Helly and have four-point half-gap growing with grid radius. | Bandelt-Chepoi host characterization and explicit quadruple; note 110 |
| SEM-858 | Rejected implication | Cayley vertex transitivity implies the Helly property. | `C4` is a vertex-transitive Cayley graph and is not Helly. | Note 110 |
| SEM-859 | Scope boundary | Standard ball-Helly claims do not automatically transfer to directed positive-alphabet reachability. | Asymmetric forward/reverse distances. | Note 110 |
| SEM-860 | Reporting rule | Distance-row throughput, pairwise ball checks, common-witness intersection, and ordinary frontier/visited throughput are distinct GPU/multi-GPU claims. | Declared graph class and output contract. | Note 110 |
| SEM-861 | Definition | A metric interval `I(u,v)` contains exactly the vertices lying on at least one shortest `u-v` path. | Connected undirected graph metric. | Note 111 |
| SEM-862 | Evidence boundary | Two endpoint distance rows test full interval membership, while one BFS tree can omit alternative-geodesic vertices. | Exact BFS distances versus selected parents. | Note 111 |
| SEM-863 | Definition | A median graph has exactly one vertex in `I(a,b) intersect I(b,c) intersect I(c,a)` for every triple. | Connected undirected graph. | Mulder; note 111 |
| SEM-864 | Classical fact | Trees, hypercubes, and Cartesian products of paths are median graphs. | Standard graph metrics. | Mulder; Bandelt-Chepoi; note 111 |
| SEM-865 | Classical fact | Every median graph is a bipartite partial cube, but not every partial cube is median. | `C6` is a partial-cube counterexample. | Bandelt-Chepoi; note 111 |
| SEM-866 | Qualified fact | In an isometric cube embedding, BFS depth is Hamming weight relative to the root, but layer counts need not be binomial. | The embedded graph may omit cube vertices. | Notes 32 and 111 |
| SEM-867 | Classical fact | A median graph's triple median is coordinatewise majority in a cube embedding. | Validated isometric median-graph embedding. | Bandelt-Hedlikova; note 111 |
| SEM-868 | Rejected equivalence | Unique triple medians and uniqueness of every weighted total-distance graph median are the same property. | Equal endpoint weights on one edge have two facility-location minimizers. | Bandelt 1992; note 111 |
| SEM-869 | Rejected implication | Median graphs are ball-Helly. | `C4` is median, but its four radius-one balls pairwise intersect with empty total intersection. | Notes 110 and 111 |
| SEM-870 | Rejected implication | Median structure implies uniformly thin triangles or small BFS frontiers. | Large grids have growing hyperbolicity; hypercubes have binomial peak frontiers. | Notes 32, 109, and 111 |
| SEM-871 | Rejected implication | Cayley vertex transitivity implies median structure. | Triangle and complete Cayley graphs fail the triple-interval condition. | Note 111 |
| SEM-872 | Validated counterexample | Median structure depends on generators even for one group. | Standard generators give `C4` for `Z2^2`; adding the diagonal generator gives non-median `K4`. | Note 111 |
| SEM-873 | Scope boundary | Schreier quotients and directed positive alphabets require direct interval/median definitions and evidence. | Changed vertex identity or asymmetric distance. | Note 111 |
| SEM-874 | Evidence boundary | Arbitrary puzzle coordinates or hashes are not partial-cube coordinates without an isometric-distance proof. | Coordinate equality alone is insufficient. | Note 111 |
| SEM-875 | Reporting rule | Distance rows, interval filters, triple intersections, embedding validation, and frontier/visited throughput are distinct GPU/multi-GPU claims. | Declared representation and output contract. | Note 111 |
| SEM-876 | Definition | `TC(u)` requires every adjacent same-layer pair to have a common neighbor one BFS layer closer to `u`. | Exact undirected distances from root `u`. | Chalopin et al.; note 112 |
| SEM-877 | Definition | `QC(u)` closes a same-layer distance-two pair with a common upper neighbor by a common lower neighbor. | Exact undirected distances and stated adjacency premises. | Chalopin et al.; note 112 |
| SEM-878 | Definition | A graph is weakly modular when TC and QC hold relative to every root. | Connected undirected graph. | Chalopin et al.; note 112 |
| SEM-879 | Classical fact | Modular graphs are bipartite weakly modular graphs and equivalently give every vertex triple at least one median. | Connected undirected graph. | Bandelt-Chepoi; note 112 |
| SEM-880 | Classical fact | Median graphs strengthen modular graphs by making every triple median unique. | Connected undirected graph. | Notes 111 and 112 |
| SEM-881 | Validated counterexample | Modularity does not imply unique medians. | Three vertices in the size-three part of `K_(2,3)` have both opposite-part vertices as medians. | Note 112 |
| SEM-882 | Validated counterexample | Weak modularity does not imply modularity. | `K3` satisfies TC/QC but is nonbipartite and its vertex triple has no median. | Note 112 |
| SEM-883 | Evidence boundary | TC/QC relative to one root do not certify weak modularity unless root symmetry transports every case. | Definition quantifies over all roots. | Note 112 |
| SEM-884 | Rejected implication | A BFS parent tree contains enough evidence to verify TC/QC. | Parent selection can omit qualifying common predecessors and nontree edges. | Note 112 |
| SEM-885 | Evidence boundary | A TC/QC violation requires exact layers, complete adjacency, and exhaustive absence evidence for the required common lower neighbor. | Fixed vertex identity and successor relation. | Note 112 |
| SEM-886 | Rejected implication | Small TC/QC diagrams make arbitrary bounded-radius sampling a global weak-modularity proof. | Local-to-global theorems require their additional topology and coverage hypotheses. | Chalopin et al.; note 112 |
| SEM-887 | Rejected implication | Weak modularity implies ball-Helly, unique medians, bounded hyperbolicity, or small frontiers. | `K3`, `C4`, large grids, and complete graphs separate the properties. | Notes 109-112 |
| SEM-888 | Qualified fact | Cayley translation reduces TC/QC root redundancy to identity-relative configurations for a fixed symmetric generating set. | Complete identity-relative distance/configuration coverage. | Note 112 |
| SEM-889 | Validated counterexample | Weak modularity class membership can change with the generator set while the group stays fixed. | `Z2^2` gives median `C4` or weakly modular nonmodular `K4`. | Note 112 |
| SEM-890 | Reporting rule | TC/QC distance construction, diagram enumeration, neighbor intersections, absence reductions, and frontier/visited throughput are distinct GPU/multi-GPU claims. | Declared universal or sampled coverage. | Note 112 |
| SEM-891 | Definition | Treewidth minimizes the largest tree-decomposition bag size minus one under coverage, edge, and running-intersection conditions. | Undirected graph. | Robertson-Seymour; note 113 |
| SEM-892 | Definition | A BFS layering partitions vertices by root distance and every edge stays within one layer or crosses to an adjacent layer. | Undirected unweighted graph. | Note 113 |
| SEM-893 | Rejected implication | Bounded treewidth implies bounded BFS frontier width. | Stars and complete binary trees have treewidth one and arbitrarily large BFS layers. | Note 113 |
| SEM-894 | Scope boundary | Treewidth is root-free while a BFS frontier profile depends on the selected root. | Fixed graph, varying source. | Note 113 |
| SEM-895 | Rejected equivalence | BFS layers are tree-decomposition bags. | Layers need not satisfy decomposition coverage/running intersection; bags may span and overlap many layers. | Note 113 |
| SEM-896 | Definition | Layered width bounds `|bag intersect layer|`, not total bag size or total layer size. | Fixed layering and tree decomposition. | Dujmovic-Morin-Wood; note 113 |
| SEM-897 | Validated counterexample | Layered width one can coexist with a frontier of size `n-1`. | Center-rooted star with one edge bag per leaf. | Note 113 |
| SEM-898 | Classical fact | Planar graphs have layered treewidth at most three. | Existential compatible layering and tree decomposition. | Dujmovic-Morin-Wood; note 113 |
| SEM-899 | Rejected implication | Bounded local treewidth bounds the number of vertices in a BFS ball. | Radius-one star balls have treewidth one and unbounded cardinality. | Eppstein; note 113 |
| SEM-900 | Evidence boundary | An existential layered-treewidth bound need not be a certificate for the operational BFS root and requires an actual validated decomposition. | Declared layering/decomposition construction. | Note 113 |
| SEM-901 | Rejected implication | Cayley symmetry supplies low treewidth. | Cycles, hypercubes, complete graphs, and expander Cayley families have different widths. | Note 113 |
| SEM-902 | Validated counterexample | Treewidth can change with generators while the group stays fixed. | `Z2^2` gives treewidth-two `C4` or treewidth-three `K4`. | Note 113 |
| SEM-903 | Evidence boundary | A decomposition's existence is not equivalent to having it for an implicit graph. | Bags and running-intersection evidence must be constructed and validated. | Note 113 |
| SEM-904 | Rejected transfer | Low treewidth automatically accelerates ordinary exact BFS. | Bag dynamic programming and frontier/visited traversal have different state and communication contracts. | Note 113 |
| SEM-905 | Reporting rule | Treewidth, layered treewidth, frontier width, separator size, bag replication, and owner load are distinct GPU/multi-GPU quantities. | Declared decomposition and BFS root. | Note 113 |
| SEM-906 | Definition | A chordal graph has no induced cycle of length at least four. | Finite simple undirected graph. | Fulkerson-Gross; note 114 |
| SEM-907 | Rejected implication | Chordality means acyclicity or unique shortest paths. | Complete graphs contain cycles; the diamond has two shortest paths between its nonadjacent pair. | Note 114 |
| SEM-908 | Classical fact | A graph is chordal exactly when it admits a perfect elimination ordering. | Finite simple undirected graph. | Fulkerson-Gross; note 114 |
| SEM-909 | Classical fact | Reversing a standard-convention LexBFS order of a chordal graph yields a PEO. | Exact LexBFS label histories. | Rose-Tarjan-Lueker; note 114 |
| SEM-910 | Evidence boundary | LexBFS returns an order on every graph; chordality still requires verification of the candidate PEO. | Complete adjacency evidence. | Note 114 |
| SEM-911 | Validated counterexample | Reversing an arbitrary ordinary BFS order of a chordal graph need not yield a PEO. | Diamond order `r,a,b,v`; later neighbors `a,b,r` of `v` are not a clique. | Note 114 |
| SEM-912 | Rejected equivalence | Sorted-adjacency FIFO BFS is LexBFS. | LexBFS priorities evolve from full selected-neighbor histories. | Notes 19 and 114 |
| SEM-913 | Scope boundary | Exact BFS distances, a PEO, and a BFS parent tree are different outputs and do not certify one another. | Declared graph and ordering convention. | Note 114 |
| SEM-914 | Rejected transfer | BFS on a chordal completion is exact BFS on the original graph. | Fill edges can shorten distances and change layers and paths. | Note 114 |
| SEM-915 | Rejected implication | Chordality bounds BFS frontier or successor work. | Complete graphs and center-rooted stars are chordal with first frontier `n-1`. | Note 114 |
| SEM-916 | Classical fact | For chordal graphs, treewidth equals maximum clique size minus one. | Finite chordal graph. | Clique-tree/PEO structure; note 114 |
| SEM-917 | Derived fact | Every finite connected simple vertex-transitive chordal graph is complete. | Finiteness, connectedness, vertex transitivity, simple undirected graph. | Simplicial-vertex transport proof; note 114 |
| SEM-918 | Cayley consequence | A finite connected simple undirected Cayley graph is chordal only when it is complete. | Standard underlying simple Cayley graph. | Vertex-transitive simplicial-vertex argument; note 114 |
| SEM-919 | Scope boundary | The finite Cayley consequence does not extend to infinite Cayley graphs or directed positive alphabets. | Infinite free-group trees and asymmetric distance. | Note 114 |
| SEM-920 | Reporting rule | LexBFS label maintenance, PEO clique checks, witness reconstruction, and frontier/visited BFS throughput are distinct GPU/multi-GPU claims. | Declared ordering and graph completeness. | Note 114 |
| SEM-921 | Proved fact | Deleting vertices or edges cannot decrease distances between retained connected vertices. | Subgraph paths are a subset of ambient paths. | Note 115 |
| SEM-922 | Definition | An isometric subgraph preserves all pairwise distances among its vertices, not only distances from one root. | Connected subgraph and ambient graph. | Note 115 |
| SEM-923 | Definition | A distance-hereditary graph makes every connected induced subgraph isometric. | Connected undirected graph. | Howorka; note 115 |
| SEM-924 | Classical equivalence | A graph is distance-hereditary exactly when every induced path is geodesic. | Connected undirected graph. | Howorka; Bandelt-Mulder; note 115 |
| SEM-925 | Validated counterexample | An induced subgraph need not preserve ambient distance. | Deleting one vertex from `C5` leaves a four-vertex path whose endpoints change distance from two to three. | Note 115 |
| SEM-926 | Proved consequence | In a connected induced subgraph of a distance-hereditary graph containing root `s`, BFS layers are exact intersections of ambient layers with retained vertices. | Connected induced subgraph. | Distance equality; note 115 |
| SEM-927 | Classical fact | Finite distance-hereditary graphs admit pendant/true-twin/false-twin pruning sequences. | Each relation checked in the current remaining graph. | Bandelt-Mulder; note 115 |
| SEM-928 | Evidence boundary | A pruning sequence is not a BFS ordering and must validate each current-graph neighborhood relation. | Exact dynamic induced graph. | Note 115 |
| SEM-929 | Classical fact | Holes of length at least five, house, gem, and domino are the forbidden induced obstructions for distance-hereditary graphs. | Finite simple undirected graph. | Bandelt-Mulder and later forbidden-subgraph characterization; note 115 |
| SEM-930 | Rejected equivalence | Chordal and distance-hereditary graphs are the same class. | `C4` is distance-hereditary nonchordal; the gem is chordal non-distance-hereditary. | Notes 114 and 115 |
| SEM-931 | Rejected implication | Twin vertices may be merged as exact BFS duplicates without changing outputs. | Named states, mutual distance, parents, multiplicities, and frontier counts can change. | Note 115 |
| SEM-932 | Validated counterexample | Cayley vertex transitivity implies distance heredity. | Standard `Z6` cycle is not distance-hereditary, while complete `Z6` Cayley graph is. | Note 115 |
| SEM-933 | Validated counterexample | Hypercube/Cayley coordinates imply distance heredity. | The three-cube contains an induced six-cycle. | Notes 111 and 115 |
| SEM-934 | Scope boundary | Standard distance-hereditary theory does not automatically transfer to Schreier quotients or directed positive alphabets. | Changed identity/neighborhoods or asymmetric distance. | Note 115 |
| SEM-935 | Reporting rule | Pruning validation, quotient lifting, structural compression, exact BFS outputs, and frontier/owner throughput are distinct GPU/multi-GPU claims. | Declared graph and output contract. | Note 115 |
| SEM-936 | Definition | A multiplicative spanner is a spanning subgraph preserving every pair distance between `d_G` and `t*d_G`. | Fixed weighted or unweighted graph metric. | Peleg-Schaffer; Althofer et al.; note 116 |
| SEM-937 | Qualified fact | Exact BFS on an unweighted spanner returns an original-graph path and an upper bound on original distance, not generally the exact original distance. | Spanner is a true subgraph. | Note 116 |
| SEM-938 | Rejected implication | Edge sparsity implies small BFS frontier or fewer levels. | A star is a sparse two-spanner of `K_n`, with center frontier `n-1` and leaf depth two. | Note 116 |
| SEM-939 | Definition | An emulator may use weighted virtual edges not present in the original graph while approximating its distances. | Same vertex set and declared no-underestimation/approximation contract. | Note 116 |
| SEM-940 | Evidence boundary | Emulator distances do not provide replay-valid original paths without virtual-edge unpacking witnesses. | Original edge or move sequence required. | Note 116 |
| SEM-941 | Definition | A hopset supplies bounded-hop weighted approximate paths after adding shortcut edges. | Declared hopbound and approximation factor. | Cohen; note 116 |
| SEM-942 | Rejected equivalence | Hop count equals original BFS distance. | One weighted shortcut can encode a long original path as one hop. | Note 116 |
| SEM-943 | Scope boundary | Spanners, emulators, hopsets, and BFS parent trees preserve different path and distance contracts. | Output and path-lifting semantics declared. | Notes 81 and 116 |
| SEM-944 | Proved Cayley fact | If every original generator has a retained-generator word of length at most `L`, the retained-generator Cayley graph is an `L`-spanner. | Symmetric `S' subset S` generating the same group. | Generator-by-generator path substitution; note 116 |
| SEM-945 | Rejected implication | A connected retained-generator graph automatically has a small stretch factor. | Connectivity gives some finite factor only for a finite graph; the bound can be large and needs proof. | Note 116 |
| SEM-946 | Evidence boundary | Finite-radius Cayley BFS observations do not prove a global generator-spanner guarantee. | Algebraic replacement words or exhaustive global evidence required. | Note 116 |
| SEM-947 | Scope boundary | Directed generator removal needs directed replacement words; unavailable inverses cannot appear in the proof. | Positive directed alphabet. | Note 116 |
| SEM-948 | Schreier boundary | Identity-state path equality may be stabilizer-specific and is not necessarily a group-word substitution valid at every state. | Nonfree group action. | Notes 16, 61, and 116 |
| SEM-949 | Exactness boundary | A replayable approximate path proves an upper bound, not optimality, until an independent lower bound matches it. | Exact-search output contract. | Note 116 |
| SEM-950 | Reporting rule | Construction, edge memory, hopbound, weighted rounds, shortcut communication, unpacking, and original exact BFS throughput are distinct GPU/multi-GPU claims. | Declared approximate structure. | Note 116 |
| SEM-951 | Definition | The doubling constant is the minimum number of half-radius balls covering every metric ball; doubling dimension is its base-two logarithm. | Ball and rounding convention declared. | Gupta-Krauthgamer-Lee; note 117 |
| SEM-952 | Proved fact | In an unweighted graph, iterated doubling covers give `|B(x,R)| <= lambda^ceil(log_2(2R))` for integer `R>=1`. | Minimum distinct-point distance one and closed half-radius convention. | Note 117 |
| SEM-953 | Proved consequence | Uniformly bounded doubling dimension implies polynomial BFS-ball and frontier growth. | Scale-independent doubling constant. | Iterated cover; note 117 |
| SEM-954 | Proved fact | An unweighted graph's doubling constant is at least maximum degree plus one. | Unit balls covered by half-radius singleton balls. | Note 117 |
| SEM-955 | Rejected counterexample | Stars or complete graphs disprove doubling-based frontier bounds. | Their large unit balls force correspondingly large doubling dimension. | Note 117 |
| SEM-956 | Evidence boundary | Doubling dimension does not determine exact frontier profiles, duplicates, path counts, or edge arrangement. | It is a worst-case covering envelope. | Note 117 |
| SEM-957 | Qualified fact | A regular infinite tree has no finite scale-independent doubling constant. | Branching factor greater than one. | Packing across growing radii; note 117 |
| SEM-958 | Qualified fact | Packing numbers in doubling metrics are bounded by `lambda^O(log(R/r))`. | Separation and open/closed convention affect constants. | Har-Peled-Mendel; note 117 |
| SEM-959 | Rejected equivalence | A metric net is an exact BFS visited quotient. | Covering proximity does not establish state identity or output-preserving lifting. | Note 117 |
| SEM-960 | Scope boundary | Every finite metric is trivially doubling with dimension at most `log_2 n`; useful claims need quantitative or family-scale bounds. | Finite `n`-point metric. | Note 117 |
| SEM-961 | Cayley symmetry fact | For a fixed symmetric Cayley metric, an identity-ball cover translates to every center. | Left translation is an isometry. | Note 117 |
| SEM-962 | Rejected invariance | Changing generators preserves the numerical doubling constant and BFS profile. | Unit balls, degree, word metric, and covers change. | Notes 93, 116, and 117 |
| SEM-963 | Evidence boundary | Cayley translation removes the center quantifier but not the requirement to cover every relevant radius. | Fixed generating set. | Note 117 |
| SEM-964 | Scope boundary | Standard doubling dimension does not directly apply to asymmetric directed reachability. | Symmetric metric required. | Note 117 |
| SEM-965 | Reporting rule | Ball construction, packing, cover validation, net storage/query, and original exact BFS throughput are distinct GPU/multi-GPU claims. | Declared scales and coverage. | Note 117 |
| SEM-966 | Definition | A replacement distance is the shortest distance in the explicitly surviving graph `G-F`; the classical replacement-paths problem separately deletes each edge of one fixed shortest `s-t` path. | Failure identity and scope declared. | Hershberger-Suri-Bhosle; note 118 |
| SEM-967 | Proved fact | Edge or vertex deletion cannot decrease surviving shortest-path distance. | Same endpoints survive; infinity denotes disconnection. | Path-set inclusion; note 118 |
| SEM-968 | Rejected universal | An ordinary BFS tree is a one-edge fault-tolerant BFS structure. | A deleted tree edge can have a replacement path through omitted non-tree edges. | Diamond counterexample; note 118 |
| SEM-969 | Qualified fact | Failure outside a selected BFS-tree path leaves that witness usable but may change multiplicity or canonical-alternative outputs. | Failed item is not used by the witness. | Note 118 |
| SEM-970 | Scope boundary | Failure of a tree edge may cause equal distance, larger finite distance, or disconnection in the full graph. | Full surviving adjacency required. | Diamond, cycle, and path calibrations; note 118 |
| SEM-971 | Proved bridge fact | A failed tree edge disconnects its subtree exactly when it is the sole original edge crossing the corresponding fundamental cut. | Complete graph and edge identity. | Notes 83 and 118 |
| SEM-972 | Evidence boundary | Original BFS distances remain lower bounds after deletion but are not generally exact surviving distances. | Deletion-only graph change. | Path-set inclusion; note 118 |
| SEM-973 | Evidence boundary | The original predecessor DAG can prove an equal-length surviving alternative but cannot exclude longer detours outside all original shortest paths. | Complete original shortest-path DAG. | Notes 53 and 118 |
| SEM-974 | Scope boundary | One-path edge replacements, all-edge sensitivity, vertex failures, sourcewise FT-BFS, all-pairs queries, and multiple failures are different output contracts. | Query and failure family declared. | Note 118 |
| SEM-975 | Definition | An exact FT-BFS subgraph preserves `d_(G-F)(s,v)` for every declared failure and surviving target. | Source set, edge/vertex model, failure budget, and exactness declared. | Parter-Peleg; note 118 |
| SEM-976 | Evidence boundary | Absence of a path in an old BFS tree or predecessor DAG does not certify post-failure disconnection. | Exhaustive surviving reachability or independent cut proof required. | Note 118 |
| SEM-977 | Cayley boundary | A local edge failure generally breaks translation symmetry, whereas global generator failure retains a reduced-alphabet Cayley/Schreier action. | Failure granularity declared. | Note 118 |
| SEM-978 | Proved Cayley fact | After global generator removal, reachable components are orbits or cosets generated by the surviving moves and may be proper subsets. | Fixed action and surviving alphabet. | `Z_6` calibration; note 118 |
| SEM-979 | Rejected equivalence | Failure of a GPU/owner is equivalent to deletion of its owned graph states. | Compute recovery must preserve the semantic graph. | Notes 30 and 118 |
| SEM-980 | Reporting rule | Failure preprocessing, retained redundancy, per-scenario work, compute recovery, and exact post-failure validation are distinct claims. | Failure family and graph epoch declared. | Note 118 |
| SEM-981 | Proved fact | A connected simple graph of maximum degree `Delta` and diameter at most `D` has order at most `1+Delta*sum_(j=0)^(D-1)(Delta-1)^j`. | Undirected simple graph. | BFS layer counting; note 119 |
